\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Gamma _Z =3 \Gamma (Z\rightarrow \nu _e \bar {\nu _e}) + 3 \Gamma (Z\rightarrow e^+ e^-) + 3 \times 2 \ \Gamma (Z\rightarrow u \bar {u}) + 3 \times 3 \ \Gamma (Z\rightarrow d \bar {d}). \label {eq:GammaZ} \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This means that the \(Z\) couples equally to the three neutrino families and the three charged lepton families. The \(Z\) boson decays to only two up-type quarks, since the decay into \(t \bar {t}\) is not allowed by kinematics, and the three bottom type quarks. Each quark type has three colour states, hence a further factor of 3.

The total width of the \(Z\) boson depends on the number and type of particles, with mass smaller than \(M_Z/2\), the \(Z\) decays to. A detailed study of the \(Z\) width can be used to calculate the number of neutrino families by comparing the cross-section of \(e^+ e^-\) collisions as a function of the centre-of-mass energy with experimental data as shown in Fig 10.4. The result of the comparison shows that the \(Z\) decays into 3 different neutrino families, which means that if an additional neutrino exists its mass has to be larger than \(M_Z/2\) or it does not have standard weak interactions.

Equation 10.35 can be used to calculate the branching ratios of the \(Z\) boson into its various decay modes. When taking the ratio

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Gamma (Z\rightarrow e^+ e^-) / \Gamma _Z, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
all the common factors relative to the phase space and the interaction vertex cancel; only the \(c_V\) and \(c_A\) factors remain. The end result is
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow e^+ e^-) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow \mu ^+ \mu ^-) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow \tau ^+ \tau ^-) \approx 3.5\% \\ & \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow \nu _e \bar {\nu _e}) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow \nu _\mu \bar {\nu _\mu }) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow \nu _\tau \bar {\nu _\tau }) \approx 6.9\% \\ & \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow u \bar {u}) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow c \bar {c}) \approx 12\% \\ & \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow d \bar {d}) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow s \bar {s}) = \mathrm {BR}(Z\rightarrow b \bar {b}) \approx 15\%. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
10.3.2 W Boson Decay

The decay rates of the W boson follow from 10.34 with \(g_X = g_W/\sqrt {2}\) and \(c_A = c_V = 1\). For the leptons, since we ignored the lepton mass differences we write:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) = \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \mu ^- \bar {\nu }_\mu ) = \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \tau ^- \bar {\nu }_\tau ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The decays into quarks are more complex. There is a factor of three due to the colour terms. But also, the electroweak quark states do not correspond to the strong quark states, but they are mixed with the CKM matrix. So we get:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {u} d) = 3 |V_{ud}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e)\\ &\Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {u} s) = 3 |V_{us}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) \\ & \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {u} b) = 3 |V_{ub}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) \\ & \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {c} d) = 3 |V_{cd}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) \\ & \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {c} s) = 3 |V_{cs}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) \\ & \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow \bar {c} b) = 3 |V_{cb}|^2 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e) \\ \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Note that decays into the top-quark are not allowed, since the top quark is heavier than the \(W\) boson. From the unitarity of the CKM matrix we get:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} |V_{ud}|^2 + |V_{us}|^2 + |V_{ub}|^2 = |V_{cd}|^2 + |V_{cs}|^2 + |V_{cb}|^2 = 1. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
In the end
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Gamma (W \rightarrow q \bar {q}') = 6 \Gamma (W^- \rightarrow e^- \bar {\nu }_e). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Therefore the total width of the \(W\) boson will be \((6+3) = 9\) times the width to \(e^- \bar {\nu }_e\) and the branching ratio of the \(W\) into hadrons is \(6/9 = 2/3 \approx 67 \%\).

\(\nextSection \)

11 Higgs Mechanism

Gauge invariance is one of the principles of the Standard Model. Moreover, gauge theories were shown to be renormalizable by ’t Hooft and Veltman in the early 1970s.

On the other hand, weak interactions seem not to be compatible with gauge invariance. Mass terms, \(\frac {1}{2} m_Z^2 Z^{\mu } Z_{\mu }\), for the gauge bosons break gauge invariance, and the same issue affects fermion mass terms since the left- and right-handed fermions belong to different representations.

A solution to this problem was discovered in the 1960s by Brout, Englert, Higgs, Guralnik, Kibble, Anderson, and others, starting from ideas first developed in condensed matter physics. Their solution was incorporated by Weinberg and Salam in their formulation of the electroweak theory and today is one of the cornerstones of the Standard Model.

In this section we will develop the main ideas in the context of Abelian theories like QED. In the next chapter we will expand our treatment to non-Abelian theories, completing our description of the SM.

11.1 Spontaneous Breaking of Global Symmetries

We consider the Lagrangian

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = \partial _{\mu } \phi ^* \partial ^{\mu } \phi - \mu ^2 \phi \phi ^* - \lambda (\phi \phi ^*)^2 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
for a complex scalar field
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \phi = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (\phi _1 + i\phi _2). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This Lagrangian has a global \(U(1)\) symmetry
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \phi \rightarrow e^{i\theta }\phi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Since

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &\left [ \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (\partial _\mu \phi _1 - i \partial _\mu \phi _2) \right ] \left [ \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (\partial ^\mu \phi _1 + i \partial ^\mu \phi _2) \right ] \\ &= \frac {1}{2} \left [ (\partial _\mu \phi _1)(\partial ^\mu \phi _1) + i(\partial _\mu \phi _1)(\partial ^\mu \phi _2) - i(\partial _\mu \phi _2)(\partial ^\mu \phi _1) - i^2(\partial _\mu \phi _2)(\partial ^\mu \phi _2) \right ]\\ & = \frac {1}{2} \partial _\mu \phi _1 \partial ^\mu \phi _1 + \frac {1}{2} \partial _\mu \phi _2 \partial ^\mu \phi _2 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We can write the Lagrangian as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = \frac {1}{2 } \partial _\mu {\phi _1} \partial ^\mu {\phi _1} + \frac {1}{2 } \partial _\mu {\phi _2} \partial ^\mu {\phi _2} - V(\phi ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The potential is
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} V(\phi ) = \mu ^2 \phi \phi ^* + \lambda (\phi \phi ^*)^2 = \frac {\mu ^2}{2} (\phi _1^2 + \phi _2^2) + \frac {\lambda }{4}(\phi _1^2 + \phi _2^2)^2. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

We can separate two cases. If \(\lambda > 0\) and \(\mu ^2 > 0\), there is an overall minimum of \(V(\phi )\) at \( \phi _1 = \phi _2 = 0\). The fields have masses equal to \(\sqrt {\mu ^2}\), and interact via vertices involving four fields with coupling constant proportional to \( \lambda \).

If \( \lambda > 0\) and \(\mu ^2 < 0\), then the configuration is unstable; the mass term has the “wrong sign” (imaginary). In this case the true vacuum is no longer at \(\phi = 0\): we obtain the Mexican-hat potential, see Figure 11.1, with a degenerate circle of minima in the \(\phi _1 - \phi _2\) plane.

Setting \(X^2 = \phi _1^2 + \phi _2^2 \), so as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} V(\phi ) = \frac {\mu ^2}{2} X^2 + \frac {\lambda }{4}X^4, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
we obtain
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {dV}{dX} = (\mu ^2X + \lambda X^3) = (\mu ^2 + \lambda X^2)X = 0, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
corresponding to a minimum at
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} X^2 = \phi _1^2 + \phi _2^2 = \frac {-\mu ^2}{\lambda } = v^2. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Without loss of generality, we give a non-zero vacuum expectation value to the \(\phi _1\) direction: \(\langle 0|\phi _1|0\rangle = v\), meaning \(\langle 0|\phi |0\rangle = v/\sqrt {2}\).

We want to understand the content of the theory after spontaneous symmetry breaking. This can be achieved by expanding the Lagrangian around the minimum:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \phi (x) = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (v + {\xi }(x) + i{\eta }(x)). \label {eq:minimum_exp_global} \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We can insert Eq. 11.10 into the \(V(\phi )\) expression:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} V(\phi ) &= \frac {\mu ^2}{2} (v^2 + \xi ^2 +2 v\xi +\eta ^2) + \frac {\lambda }{4} (v^2 + \xi ^2 +2 v\xi +\eta ^2)^2 \\ &= \frac {\mu ^2}{2} (v^2 + \xi ^2 +2 v\xi +\eta ^2) \\&- \frac {\mu ^2}{4v^2} (v^4 +4v^3\xi +6v^2\xi ^2 +2v^2\eta ^2 +4v\xi ^3 +4v\xi \eta ^2 +\xi ^4 +2\xi ^2\eta ^2 +\eta ^4) \\ &= -\mu ^2 \xi ^2 -\frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi ^3 -\frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi \eta ^2 - \frac {\mu ^2}{4v^2}\xi ^4 -\frac {\mu ^2}{2v^2}\xi ^2\eta ^2 -\frac {\mu ^2} {4v^2}\eta ^4 +\frac {\mu ^2 v^2}{4}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Substituting into the Lagrangian gives:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = \frac {1}{2} \partial _{\mu } \xi \partial ^{\mu } \xi &+ \frac {1}{2} \partial _{\mu } \eta \partial ^{\mu } \eta + \mu ^2 \xi ^2 + \frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi ^3 +\frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi \eta ^2 \\ &+\frac {\mu ^2}{4v^2}\xi ^4 +\frac {\mu ^2}{2v^2}\xi ^2\eta ^2 +\frac {\mu ^2} {4v^2}\eta ^4 -\frac {\mu ^2 v^2}{4}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We can identify a mass term \(\mu ^2 \xi ^2 = - \frac {1}{2} m_\xi ^2 \xi ^2\) with \(m_\xi = \sqrt {-2 \mu ^2}\); recall \(\mu ^2 < 0\). The cubic terms proportional to \(\mu ^2/v\) correspond to \(\xi \xi \xi \) and \( \xi \eta \eta \) interaction vertices, while the quartic terms proportional to \(\mu ^2/v^2\) correspond to \(\xi \xi \xi \xi \), \( \xi \xi \eta \eta \), and \(\eta \eta \eta \eta \) interaction vertices.

We started with a complex field possessing \(U(1)\) symmetry, representing two physical degrees of freedom. After spontaneous symmetry breaking, we have two scalar bosons, so again two degrees of freedom as there should be. We have a massive \(\xi \) scalar boson along the direction we broke the symmetry, and we also have a massless scalar particle \(\eta \).

The breaking of a continuous internal global symmetry implies the existence of massless scalar particles. This is Goldstone’s theorem and the massless particles are known as Goldstone bosons. There is one Goldstone boson for each broken internal symmetry generator. But there is a further twist in the story: the Goldstone boson can be “evaded” [22] in a gauge theory.

11.2 Higgs Mechanism for Abelian Gauge Theory

We consider a QED-like Lagrangian of the form

\begin{equation} \label {eq:before_breaking} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = D_{\mu } \phi ^* D^{\mu } \phi - \mu ^2 \phi \phi ^* - \lambda (\phi \phi ^*)^2 -\frac {1}{4} F^{\mu \nu } F_{\mu \nu } \end {aligned} \end{equation}
possessing local \(U(1)\) symmetry with
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_\mu \phi = (\partial _\mu + iQeA_\mu ) \phi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = (\partial _\mu - iQeA_\mu ) \phi ^* (\partial ^\mu + iQeA^\mu ) \phi - \mu ^2 \phi \phi ^* - \lambda (\phi \phi ^*)^2 -\frac {1}{4} F^{\mu \nu } F_{\mu \nu }. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Proceeding as in the previous section we substitute

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \phi (x) = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (v + {\xi }(x) + i{\eta }(x)). \label {eq:scalar_expansion_gauge} \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Expanding the kinetic term \(|D_\mu \phi |^2\) first:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} |D_\mu \phi |^2 = & \frac {1}{2} \left [ (\partial _\mu \xi - Qe A_\mu \eta )^2 + (\partial _\mu \eta + Qe A_\mu (v + \xi ))^2 \right ] \\ = & \frac {1}{2}(\partial _\mu \xi )^2 + \frac {1}{2}(\partial _\mu \eta )^2 + \frac {1}{2}Q^2 e^2 v^2 A_\mu A^\mu + Qe v A^\mu \partial _\mu \eta \\ & + Qe A^\mu (\xi \partial _\mu \eta - \eta \partial _\mu \xi ) + Q^2 e^2 v \xi A_\mu A^\mu + \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 (\xi ^2 + \eta ^2) A_\mu A^\mu . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The full Lagrangian is:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = & \frac {1}{2} \partial _{\mu } \xi \partial ^{\mu } \xi + \frac {1}{2} \partial _{\mu } \eta \partial ^{\mu } \eta + \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 v^2 A^\mu A_\mu + Qe v A^\mu \partial _\mu \eta - \frac {1}{4} F^{\mu \nu } F_{\mu \nu } \\ & + \mu ^2 \xi ^2 + \frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi ^3 + \frac {\mu ^2}{v}\xi \eta ^2 + \frac {\mu ^2}{4v^2}\xi ^4 + \frac {\mu ^2}{2v^2}\xi ^2\eta ^2 + \frac {\mu ^2}{4v^2}\eta ^4 \\ & + Qe A^\mu (\xi \partial _\mu \eta - \eta \partial _\mu \xi ) + Q^2 e^2 v \xi A_\mu A^\mu + \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 (\xi ^2 + \eta ^2) A_\mu A^\mu - \frac {\mu ^2 v^2}{4}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

  • There is a mass term for the \(A_\mu \) field: \(\frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 v^2 A^\mu A_\mu = \frac {1}{2} M_A^2 A^\mu A_\mu \).

  • We still have a scalar particle \(\xi \) with mass \(m_\xi = \sqrt {-2 \mu ^2}\).

Let’s count physical degrees of freedom. Before spontaneous symmetry breaking we have two polarization states for the massless \(A^\mu \) field. There are in addition two scalar fields (or a single complex one). So we have four degrees of freedom. After spontaneous symmetry breaking we have a massive scalar boson \(\xi \), and the \(A^\mu \) field is now massive, so it should acquire an additional longitudinal polarization degree of freedom. If we also count the Goldstone boson \(\eta \) as a separate physical degree of freedom we end up with five degrees of freedom, one too many. The same problem can be seen in the form of the \(Qe v A^\mu \partial _\mu \eta \) term, as it is a bilinear term mixing two different fields, implying there is a vertex that turns the one field into the other.

The solution is that the Goldstone scalar field \(\eta \) is a “spurious” field which can be gauged away. We note that

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 v^2 A^\mu A_\mu + \frac {1}{2}(\partial _\mu \eta )^2 - QevA^\mu \partial _\mu \eta = \\ & \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 v^2 \left [ A_\mu A^\mu - \frac {2}{Qev} A^\mu \partial _\mu \eta + \frac {1}{Q^2 e^2 v^2} (\partial _\mu \eta )^2 \right ] = \\ &\frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 v^2 \left ( A_\mu - \frac {1}{ev} \partial _\mu \eta \right ) \left ( A^\mu - \frac {1}{ev} \partial ^\mu \eta \right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
and that \(A_\mu \) is a gauge field.

Using the freedom of gauge transformations we can perform the following substitutions:

\begin{equation} A_\mu \rightarrow A_\mu - \frac {1}{ev} \partial _\mu \eta , \quad \phi \rightarrow \phi e^{i{Q\eta }(x)/v}, \end{equation}
and all \(\eta \) terms disappear from the Lagrangian.

This choice of gauge, for which only the physical particles remain in the Lagrangian is called the unitary gauge. In this gauge let’s rename the physical Higgs field to \(h\) and the gauge field to \(V_\mu \).

After gauging away \(\eta \), the field strength remains invariant:

\begin{equation} -\frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu } = -\frac {1}{4} (\partial _\mu V_\nu - \partial _\nu V_\mu )(\partial ^\mu V^\nu - \partial ^\nu V^\mu ). \end{equation}
The complete Lagrangian density for the physical degrees of freedom \(h\) and \(V_\mu \) becomes:
\begin{equation} \label {eq:after_breaking} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = & \frac {1}{2} \partial _\mu h \partial ^\mu h - \frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu } + \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 (v+h)^2 V_\mu V^\mu - \lambda \left (vh + \frac {h^2}{2}\right )^2 \\ = & \frac {1}{2} (\partial _\mu h \partial ^\mu h + 2\mu ^2 h^2) - \frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu } + \frac {1}{2} M_V^2 V_\mu V^\mu \\ & + Q^2 e^2 v h V_\mu V^\mu + \frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 h^2 V_\mu V^\mu - \lambda v h^3 - \frac {\lambda }{4} h^4. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

From this Lagrangian, we can “read-off” the propagators and the interaction vertices of the theory in the unitary gauge. The propagators for the Higgs scalar \(h\) and the massive vector \(V_\mu \) are:

  • Higgs Scalar:

    \begin{equation*} \frac {i}{k^2 - m_h^2 + i\epsilon } \end{equation*}

  • Massive Vector:

    \begin{equation*} \frac {-i \left ( g_{\mu \nu } - \frac {p_\mu p_\nu }{M_V^2} \right )}{k^2 - M_V^2 + i\epsilon } \end{equation*}

The interaction terms between the Higgs and the vector field, as well as Higgs self-interactions terms, are:

  • \(hVV\): \(Q^2 e^2 v h V_\mu V^\mu \)

  • \(hhVV\): \(\frac {1}{2} Q^2 e^2 h^2 V_\mu V^\mu \)

  • \(hhh\): \(-\lambda v h^3\)

  • \(hhhh\): \(-\frac {\lambda }{4} h^4\)

Finally, the fermion maintains the same interaction with \(V_\mu \) that it previously had with the massless \(A_\mu \).

\begin{equation} - Q e \bar {\psi } \gamma ^\mu \psi V_\mu . \end{equation}

The Lagrangians 11.13 and 11.22 describe exactly the same physical system; all we have done is to select a convenient gauge and rewrite the fields in terms of fluctuations about a particular ground state. We have sacrificed the manifest symmetry of 11.13 in favour of a notation that “hides” the symmetry but makes the physical content more transparent, and allows us to extract the Feynman rules more directly.

\(\nextSection \)

12 Completing the SM. Non-Abelian Symmetries (*)

QED is an Abelian theory based on \(U(1)\) gauge invariance. In 1954 Yang and Mills extended the concept of gauge invariance to theories with \(SU(N)\) symmetry, where the underlying group is non-Abelian. Their motivation was the \(SU(2)\) isospin symmetry between the proton and the neutron.

Gauge theories require massless, interaction-carrying vector bosons. No such particles were known at that time. Considerable effort was therefore devoted to constructing theories with massive vector bosons. When these efforts eventually bore fruit, in the form of what is today known as the “Higgs” mechanism, it was already clear that the proton and the neutron were not elementary particles.

Nevertheless, non-Abelian gauge theories eventually found important applications in the context of the \(SU(3)\) colour symmetry of QCD and the \(SU(2)_L \times U(1)_Y\) symmetry of the electroweak theory. As a result, these ideas now play a fundamental role in the formulation of the Standard Model of particle physics.

12.1 QCD as a Yang-Mills Theory.

In QCD, the free Lagrangian for one quark flavour has the form

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = \bar {\psi }_i \left (i\gamma ^\mu \partial _\mu - m\right )\psi _i. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

In what follows, I will use a notation where Roman subscripts/superscripts near the middle of the alphabet \((i,j,k\dots )\) run from 1 to 3, denoting the three colour indices:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \psi = \left ( \begin {array}{c} \psi _1 \\ \psi _2 \\ \psi _3 \end {array} \right ), \quad \bar {\psi } = \left ( \begin {array}{ccc} \bar {\psi }_1 & \bar {\psi }_2 & \bar {\psi }_3 \end {array} \right ). \label {eq:Yang_Mills_fermions} \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Notice that \(\psi \) above is a three-component column vector in colour space whose entries are 4-component Dirac spinors. The \(\gamma ^\mu \) acts on the four components of the individual spinors. Namely if we include explicit spinor indices \(p\), \(q\)
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = \bar {\psi }_{ip} \left ( i {\gamma ^\mu }_{pq} \partial _\mu - m \delta _{pq} \right ) \psi _{iq}. \end{equation}

We would like to generalize the same gauge invariance principle employed for QED, but with the \(U(1)\) gauge group replaced by the \(SU(3)_c\) group of phase transformations on the quark colour fields. For \(U(1)\) in QED, we had

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} U = e^{iQ\theta (x)}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We generalize this to
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \psi \rightarrow \psi ' = U \psi , \end {aligned} \end{equation}
or, in a form that makes the \(N\times N\) matrix indices explicit:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \psi _i \rightarrow \psi '_i = {U_i}^j \psi _j, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(U\) is a unitary matrix. An \(SU(N)\) group has \(N^2 - 1\) generators \(t^a\) which are traceless Hermitian matrices. Therefore, for \(SU(3)_c\):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} U = e^{i \theta ^{a}(x) t^a}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(\theta ^a t^a = \sum _{a=1}^8 \theta ^a t^a\). Roman subscripts/superscripts near the beginning of the alphabet \((a,b,c)\) run from 1 to 8.

The generators \(t^a\) are defined as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} t^a = \frac {1}{2} \lambda ^a, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
with \(\lambda ^a\) being the eight Gell-Mann matrices:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &\lambda ^1 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^2 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & -i & 0 \\ i & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^3 = \begin {pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^4 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix},\\ \quad &\lambda ^5 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & -i \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ i & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^6 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^7 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -i \\ 0 & i & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad \lambda ^8 = \frac {1}{\sqrt {3}} \begin {pmatrix} 1 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -2 \end {pmatrix}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The structure constants \(f^{abc}\) are defined by \([t^a,t^b]=if^{abc}t^c\), where the non-zero values are:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &f^{123} = 1, \\ &f^{147} = -f^{156} = f^{246} = f^{257} = f^{345} = -f^{367} = \tfrac {1}{2}, \\ &f^{458} = f^{678} = \tfrac {\sqrt {3}}{2}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Other values follow from the total antisymmetry of \(f^{abc}\).

For a global transformation (independent of \(x\)):

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \psi '_{iq} &= U_{ij}\,\psi _{jq}, \\ \bar {\psi }'_{qi} &= (U_{ij}\,\psi _{jp})^\dagger (\gamma ^0)_{pq} = \psi ^\dagger _{pj}\,(U^\dagger )_{ji} (\gamma ^0)_{pq}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The unitary matrix \(U\) acts on the colour indices \((i,j)\), while the Dirac matrix \(\gamma ^0\) acts on the spinor indices \((p,q)\). Because these are independent indices, we can rearrange the components:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \bar {\psi }'_{qi} = \left ( \psi ^\dagger _{pj}(\gamma ^0)_{pq} \right ) (U^\dagger )_{ji} = \bar {\psi }_{qj}(U^\dagger )_{ji}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Therefore, summing over colour \(i\) and spinor \(q\)
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \bar {\psi }' \psi ' &= \sum _{i,q} \bar {\psi }'_{qi} \psi '_{iq} \\ &= \bar {\psi }_{qj}(U^\dagger )_{ji} U_{ik} \psi _{kq} \\ &= \bar {\psi }_{qj} (U^\dagger U)_{jk} \psi _{kq}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Since \(U\) is unitary, \((U^\dagger U)_{jk} = \delta _{jk}\):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \bar {\psi }' \psi ' &= \bar {\psi }_{qj} \delta _{jk} \psi _{kq} \\ &= \bar {\psi }_{qk} \psi _{kq} = \bar {\psi }\psi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

However, if \(U\) is a function of \(x\), a term proportional to \(\partial U\) will spoil the local invariance. To construct a gauge-invariant Lagrangian, we introduce eight gauge fields \(G_\mu ^a\), one for each generator—to compensate for the phase differences. These are the 8 gluons. We now construct the covariant derivative:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_\mu = \partial _\mu + ig_s G_{\mu }, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(G_{\mu } = G_{\mu }^a t^a = \sum \limits _{a} G_{\mu }^a t^a\) meaning
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_\mu = \partial _\mu + ig_s G_{\mu }^a t^a. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Or in a form that tries to make the \(N \times N\) matrix indices \(i\), \(j\) explicit
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} {D_\mu }_{ij} = \partial _\mu \delta _{ij} + ig_s G_{\mu }^a t^{a}_{ij}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_\mu \psi _i = \partial _\mu \psi _i + ig_s G_{\mu }^a t^{a}_{ij} \psi _j, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(t^{a}_{ij}\) is the \(ij\) element of the matrix \(t^a\). Note that the \(g_s\) and \(t^{a}\) play the roles of the QED \(e\) and \(Q\) respectively. Recall that the covariant derivative has to transform the same way as the \(\psi \)
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_\mu \psi \rightarrow (D_\mu \psi )^\prime = U D_\mu \psi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

We can use this property to derive the transformation \(G_{\mu } \rightarrow G_{\mu }^{\prime } \). We have

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (D_\mu \psi )^\prime &= (\partial _\mu + ig_s G_{\mu }^{\prime a} t^a)U\psi \\ &= (\partial _\mu U) \psi + U\partial _\mu \psi + ig_s G_{\mu }^{\prime a} t^a U\psi , \end {aligned} \end{equation}
and
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} U D_\mu \psi & = U (\partial _\mu + ig_s G_\mu ^a t^a)\psi \\ &= U\partial _\mu \psi + U ig_s G_\mu ^a t^a \psi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Equating these results:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &(\partial _\mu U) \psi + U\partial _\mu \psi + ig_s G_{\mu }^{\prime a} t^a U\psi = U\partial _\mu \psi + U ig_s G_\mu ^a t^a \psi \implies \\ &ig_s G_{\mu }^{\prime a} t^a U = U ig_s G_\mu ^a t^a - (\partial _\mu U) \implies \\ &ig_s G_{\mu }^{ \prime a } t^a = U ig_s G_\mu ^a t^a U^{-1} - (\partial _\mu U) U^{-1} \implies \\ &G_{\mu }^{ \prime a } t^a = U G_\mu ^a t^a U^{-1} - \frac {1}{ig_s} (\partial _\mu U) U^{-1}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Since \(U\) is unitary (\(U^{-1}=U^\dagger \)):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} G_{\mu }^{\prime } = U G_\mu U^\dagger + \frac {i}{g_s} (\partial _\mu U) U^\dagger . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The second term is analogous to QED, while the first term is unique to non-Abelian theories.

The final element required to complete the theory is the field strength tensor \(G_{\mu \nu }=G_{\mu \nu }^a t^a\). It is obtained via the commutator:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} G_{\mu \nu } = \frac {1}{ig_s} [D_\mu ,D_\nu ] = \partial _\mu G_\nu - \partial _\nu G_\mu + ig_s [G_\mu ,G_\nu ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Expanding in terms of the generators:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} G_{\mu \nu } &= G_{\mu \nu }^a t^a = \partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a t^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a t^a + ig_s G_{\mu }^a G_{\nu }^b [t^a,t^b] \\ &= \partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a t^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a t^a + ig_s G_{\mu }^ a G_{\nu }^b if^{abc} t^c \\ &= \partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a t^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a t^a - g_s G_{\mu }^c G_{\nu }^b f^{bca} t^a \\ & = (\partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a - g_s G_{\mu }^b G_{\nu } ^c f^{bca}) t^a. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Therefore, the components of the field strength tensor are:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} G_{\mu \nu }^a = \partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a - g_s f^{abc} G_{\mu }^b G_{\nu }^c. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

As a matter of notation notice that we took the \(\partial _\mu \), \(D_\mu \), \(G^\mu \) and \(G_{\mu \nu }\) to act on the fields in 12.2, meaning that there is always a \(3 \times 3\) \(t^a\) matrix, with the relevant \(t_{ij}\) elements, on the right-hand side of their definitions.

The full QCD Lagrangian is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{\mathrm {QCD}} = \bar {\psi }_i^A \left (i \gamma ^\mu \partial _\mu - m_A \right ) \psi _i^A - \frac {1}{4} G_{\mu \nu }^a G^{a\,\mu \nu } + g_s \, \bar {\psi }_i^A \gamma ^\mu (t^a)_{ij} \psi _j^A \, G_\mu ^a\ , \end{equation}
where \(i,j\) run over the colour indices, \(A,B\) run over the quark flavours \(u,d,s,c,b,t\) and \(t_a\) are the generators of \(SU(3)_c\). This Lagrangian contains a gluon-quark interaction term analogous to QED:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} g_s G^a_\mu \bar {\psi }^{i,A} \gamma ^\mu t^a_{ij} \psi ^{j,A}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
. The Lagrangian also contains a pure gluon part
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} - \frac {1}{4} G^{\mu \nu }_a G^a_{\mu \nu } \end {aligned} \end{equation}
with
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} G_{\mu \nu }^a = \partial _\mu G_{\nu }^a - \partial _\nu G_{\mu }^a - g_s f^{abc} G_{\mu }^b G_{\nu }^c. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The term \(G^{\mu \nu }_a G^a_{\mu \nu }\) has a remarkable new property. It contains not only the standard kinetic term of the gauge fields, but also an interaction vertex with three gauge bosons proportional to \(g_s\), and a vertex with four gauge bosons proportional to \(g_s^2\). These extra terms do not have QED analogues and are unique to Yang-Mills theories. In the context of QCD they correspond to three and four gluon vertices.

12.2 Higgs Mechanism in the Standard Model

We start from a model that has two kinds of fields. There are the matter fields, that is, the three generations of left-handed and right-handed chiral quarks and leptons. As we have seen the left-handed fields are arranged in weak doublets while the right-handed ones are arranged in singlets.

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} L_i =& \left ( \begin {array}{c} \nu _e \\ e \end {array} \right )_L \left ( \begin {array}{c} \nu _{\mu } \\ \mu \end {array} \right )_L \left ( \begin {array}{c} \nu _{\tau } \\ \tau \ \end {array} \right )_L \left ( \begin {array}{c} u \\ d \end {array} \right )_L \left ( \begin {array}{c} c \\ s \end {array} \right )_L \left ( \begin {array}{c} t \\ b \end {array} \right )_L \\ e_{Ri} =&e_R,\mu _R,\tau _R,u_R,d_R,s_R,c_R,b_R,t_R \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Then there are the gauge fields corresponding to the spin-one bosons that mediate the interactions. We have the weak hypercharge field \(B_\mu \) which corresponds to the generator \(Y\) of the \(U(1)_Y\) group and the three fields \(W_\mu ^1 \ , W_\mu ^2 \ , W_\mu ^3\) which correspond to the generators \(T_i = \frac {1}{2} \tau _i\) of the weak isospin group.

The relevant covariant derivative is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} D_{\mu } = \partial _\mu - i g_W T_a {W_\mu }^a - ig'\frac {Y}{2}B_\mu . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The SM Lagrangian before symmetry breaking (excluding QCD and quarks) is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L} = - \frac {1}{4} W_{\mu \nu }^\alpha W^{\mu \nu }_\alpha - \frac {1} {4} B_{\mu \nu } B^{\mu \nu } + \bar {L}_i i D_\mu \gamma ^\mu L_i + \bar {e}_{Ri} i D_\mu \gamma ^\mu e_{Ri}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This Lagrangian has no mass terms. The \(W_\mu ^\alpha \), \(B_\mu \) and the fermions are massless.

We now introduce a doublet of scalar fields

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left ( \begin {array}{c} \phi ^+ \\ \phi ^0 \end {array} \right ) \ = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}}\left ( \begin {array}{c} \phi _1 + i\phi _2 \\ \phi _3 + i\phi _4\end {array} \right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The doublet has weak isospin \(T=1/2\) and hypercharge \(Y = 1\) leading to the usual electromagnetic charges +1,0, for the \(\pm 1/2\) upper and lower members of the doublet. We then proceed, as before, to write a Lagrangian
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L}_\Phi = D_{\mu } \Phi ^\dagger D^{\mu } \Phi - \mu ^2 \Phi \Phi ^\dagger - \lambda (\Phi \Phi ^\dagger )^2. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
As before for \(\mu ^2 < 0\) we have a minimum at
\begin{equation*} \frac {-\mu ^2}{\lambda } . \end{equation*}

The neutral component of the doublet field \(\Phi \) will develop a vacuum expectation value (vev) at the minimum. The vev should not be in the charged direction to preserve \(U(1)_{\mathrm {QED}}\). We shall choose the vev’s of the fields \(\phi _1\), \(\phi _2\) and \(\phi _4\) to be zero, while

\begin{equation*} \bra {0}\phi _3\ket {0}^2 = v^2 = \frac {-\mu ^2}{\lambda }. \end{equation*}
We should be able to pick the vacuum direction completely arbitrarily, but in order for the photon to remain massless after the spontaneous symmetry breaking, we need to give a non-zero vev to a neutral field. To do things generally one can assign charges and other quantum numbers after performing the symmetry breaking. We will continue with a particular choice.

We now, as in the previous chapter, expand \(\Phi \) around this chosen vacuum, setting \(\phi _3 = H + v\), where \(H\) is the neutral scalar Higgs field. We will work directly in the unitary gauge in which case we can write

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Phi = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}}\left ( \begin {array}{c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This means we do not need to keep explicit track of the Goldstone fields \(\phi _1\), \(\phi _2\), \(\phi _4\).

First we evaluate \(|D_\mu \Phi |^2\) explicitly:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} |D_\mu \Phi |^2 = \frac {1}{2} \Big | \left (\partial _\mu - i g_W \frac {\tau _a}{2} {W_\mu }^a - ig'\frac {Y}{2}B_\mu \right ) \Phi \Big |^2. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the explicit form of the \(\tau \) matrices,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} |D_\mu \Phi |^2 &= \frac {1}{2} \bigg | \left (\begin {array}{cc } \partial _\mu -\frac {i}{2}(gW^3_\mu + g'B_\mu )& -\frac {ig}{2}(W^1_\mu -iW^2_\mu ) \\ -\frac {ig}{2}(W^1_\mu +iW^2_\mu ) & \partial _\mu +\frac {i}{2}(gW^3_\mu - g'B_\mu ) \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ) \bigg |^2 \\ & = \frac {1}{2} (\partial _\mu H)^2 + \frac {1}{8} g^2 (v+H)^2 | W^1_\mu +iW^2_\mu |^2 + \frac {1}{8} (v+H)^2 |g W^3_\mu - g'B_\mu |^2 . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The Vector Bosons We identify the terms bilinear to the fields

\begin{equation} \frac {1}{8} g^2 v^2 |W^1_\mu +iW^2_\mu |^2 + \frac {1}{8} v^2 |g W^3_\mu - g'B_\mu |^2. \end{equation}
Then we define new fields that correspond to the physical vector bosons. The \(W^\pm \) can be identified as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} W^{\pm } = \frac {1}{\sqrt {2}} (W^{(1)} \mp iW^{(2)}), \end {aligned} \end{equation}
leading to
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {1}{2} v^2g^2 W^+_\mu W^{-\mu }. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
So the \(W^{\pm }\) acquire mass
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} M_W = \frac {1}{2} vg. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
For the neutral vector bosons we write
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {1}{8} v^2 \left (\begin {array}{cc} W^3 & B \end {array} \right ) \left (\begin {array}{cc} g^2 & -gg\prime \\ -gg^\prime & g^{\prime 2} \end {array} \right ) \left (\begin {array}{c} W^3 \\ B \end {array} \right ), \end {aligned} \end{equation}
and we diagonalize the mass matrix
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} M^2 = \left (\begin {array}{cc} g^2 & -gg\prime \\ -gg^\prime & g^{\prime 2} \end {array} \right ) . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The determinant of the above matrix is zero. So one of the eigenvalues is zero and the other is \(g^2+g'^2\). In the diagonal basis we write
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {1}{8} v^2 \left (\begin {array}{cc} A_\mu & Z_\mu \end {array} \right ) \left (\begin {array}{cc} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & g^2+g'^2 \end {array} \right ) \left (\begin {array}{c} A_\mu \\ Z_\mu \end {array} \right ), \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where the new fields, corresponding to the normalised eigenvectors, are
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} Z_\mu = \frac {gW^3_\mu - g' B_\mu }{\sqrt {g^2+g'^2}}, \ A_\mu = \frac {gW^3_\mu + g' B_\mu }{\sqrt {g^2+g'^2}}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
with masses
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} M_Z = \frac {1}{2} v \sqrt {g^2+g'^2} , \ M_A =0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The W and Z bosons have acquired masses, while the photon is still massless.

Writing the ratio of the \(SU(2)_L\) and \(U(1)_Y\) coupling constants as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \tan {\theta }_W = \frac {g'}{g}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The relationship between the physical and underlying fields can be written as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & A =\cos {\theta }_W B + \sin {\theta }_W W^3, \\ & Z =-\sin {\theta }_WB + \cos {\theta }_W W^3. \\ \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We also have
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \cos {\theta }_W = \frac {M_W}{M_Z}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
As a result the SM fixes the relative strength of the weak and neutral weak currents
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \rho = \frac {M_W}{M_Z \cos {\theta }_W } = 1. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The Higgs Boson Turning our attention to the Higgs boson itself. The kinetic part of the Higgs field, \(\frac {1}{2}(\partial _\mu )^2\) comes from the term involving the covariant derivative as seen above. The mass and self-interaction parts, come from the scalar potential

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} V(\Phi ) &= \mu ^2 \Phi \Phi ^\dagger + \lambda (\Phi \Phi ^\dagger )^2 \\ & = \frac {\mu ^2}{2} \left ( \begin {array}{cc} 0 & H+v \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ) + \frac {\lambda }{4} \bigg | \left ( \begin {array}{cc} 0 & H+v \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ) \bigg |^2 \\ &= -\frac {\lambda }{2}v^2 ( H+v)^2 + \frac {\lambda }{4} (H+v)^4, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where in the last step we used \(\mu ^2 =- \lambda v^2\). We then perform all multiplications and at the end we omit constant terms, to obtain
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} V(\Phi ) & = -\frac {\lambda }{2}v^2 (H^2 + 2Hv + v^2) + \frac {\lambda }{4} (H^4 +4H^3v+6v^2H^2+4v^3H+v^4)\\ & = -\frac {\lambda }{2} (v^2H^2 + 2Hv^3) + \frac {\lambda }{4} (H^4 +4H^3v+6v^2H^2+4v^3H)\\ & = \lambda v^2 H^2 + \lambda v H^3 + \frac {\lambda }{4} H^4. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L}_H & = \frac {1}{2}(\partial _\mu )^2 - \lambda v^2 H^2 - \lambda v H^3 - \frac {\lambda }{4} H^4. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
From this Lagrangian one can simply read the Higgs boson mass as \(M_H^2 = 2 \lambda v^2 = - 2 \mu ^2 \). We also see that the Higgs boson has triple and quartic self-couplings.

We can again count the degrees of freedom before and after spontaneous symmetry breaking. Before spontaneous symmetry breaking we have 12 degrees of freedom: four degrees of freedom from the complex scalar doublet \(\phi \), two degrees of freedom from the massless \(B\) field (\(U(1)\) gauge boson), and six degrees of freedom from the three massless \(W^i\) bosons (\(SU(2)\) gauge bosons). After spontaneous symmetry breaking, we also have 12 degrees of freedom: one from the massive real scalar \(h\) (the Higgs boson), nine from the three massive vector bosons (\(W^\pm \) and \(Z\)) and two from the massless photon.

Fermion Masses We can also generate the fermion masses using the same scalar field \(\Phi \) with hypercharge \(Y=1\).

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L}_F & = - \lambda _e \bar {L} \Phi e_R - \lambda _d \bar {Q} \Phi d_R - \lambda _u \bar {Q} \tilde {\Phi } u_R + \mathrm {h.c}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(\tilde {\Phi } = i\tau _2 \Phi ^*\) has hypercharge \(Y=-1\) and we ignored the mass of the neutrino.

For a single generation of leptons

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L}_F & = - \frac {\lambda _e}{\sqrt {2}} \left ( \begin {array} {c} \bar {\nu }_{eL} \bar {e}_L \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array} {c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ) e_R \\ & = - \frac {\lambda _e}{\sqrt {2}}(v+H) \bar {e}_L e_R \dots + \mathrm {h.c}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(\mathrm {h.c}\) stands for Hermitian conjugate. We identify the mass as
\begin{equation} m_e = \lambda _e v/\sqrt {2} \end{equation}
and the lepton Higgs coupling
\begin{equation} \lambda _e/\sqrt {2} = m_e/v \end{equation}

For a single generation of quarks.

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {L}_F & = - \frac {\lambda _d}{\sqrt {2}} \left ( \begin {array}{cc} \bar {u}_L & \bar {d}_L \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{c} 0 \\ H+v \end {array} \right ) d_R \\ & \quad - \frac {\lambda _u}{\sqrt {2}} \left ( \begin {array}{cc} \bar {u}_L & \bar {d}_L \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{c} H+v \\ 0 \end {array} \right ) u_R \\ & = - \frac {\lambda _d}{\sqrt {2}}(v+H)\bar {d}_L d_R - \frac {\lambda _u}{\sqrt {2}}(v+H)\bar {u}_L u_R + \mathrm {h.c}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
We identify the quark masses as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} m_d &= \frac {\lambda _d v}{\sqrt {2}}, \\ m_u &= \frac {\lambda _u v}{\sqrt {2}}, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
and the corresponding Higgs couplings as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {\lambda _d}{\sqrt {2}} &= \frac {m_d}{v}, \\ \frac {\lambda _u}{\sqrt {2}} &= \frac {m_u}{v}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

12.3 Higgs Discovery and Current Status

Most of the theoretical concepts in the Standard Model were in place by the end of the 1960s. These ideas gained strong support with the discovery of the W and Z bosons at CERN in the mid 1980s. In the last decade of the twentieth century, precision studies of the W and Z bosons provided stringent tests of the Standard Model predictions. The discovery of the Higgs boson by the ATLAS and CMS collaborations in 2012 completed the particle content of the Standard Model.

It is hard to believe that the Standard Model is the final theory of particle physics. It is a framework constructed from a number of beautiful and profound ideas, put together in a somewhat ad hoc fashion, and supported by a wide body of experimental results that guided its construction.

Precision tests of the Standard Model and the discovery of the Higgs boson have firmly established its validity at energies up to the electroweak scale. Despite this success, many questions remain unanswered. We may be standing at the threshold of new and potentially revolutionary discoveries. This could be the direct detection of dark matter, an understanding of the nature of neutrino mass, the observation of deviations from Standard Model predictions at the LHC or quite possibly something completely unexpected.

\(\nextSection \)

Appendices

A More on Index Notation

A tensor is a geometric object defined in spacetime, whose components carry indices and transform appropriately under a change of reference frame. One of the difficulties in working with tensors and index notation is that we are often used to particular coordinate systems, so we sometimes overlook actual geometric properties. I will adopt a practical approach. Meaning that I will avoid certain mathematical terms and formal definitions.

A.1 Points and Coordinates

A point represents a physical location in space, such as a specific spot in this room. Coordinates are simply labels assigned to that point when we describe its position within a coordinate system. Different coordinate systems can be used to describe the same point. Importantly, the point itself does not change when we change coordinates; only its labels do.

Suppose a point \(P\) is described by coordinates \(x\) in one coordinate system and by \(x'\) in another. If we define a scalar function on space, its value at the point \(P\) must remain the same regardless of the coordinate system used. Thus

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Phi (x) = \Phi '(x'). \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Here \(\Phi (x)\) and \(\Phi '(x')\) represent the same function written in two different coordinate systems. As an example, suppose the function in the original coordinates is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Phi (x) = x_1 + x_2 . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Now consider a change of coordinates defined by
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} x' = 3x . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Because the point \(P\) has not moved, the value of the function must remain the same. Therefore the function written in the new coordinates must be
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \Phi '(x') = \frac {1}{3}x_1' + \frac {1}{3}x_2' . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

A.2 Vectors and the Metric

A vector is commonly described as an object possessing both a magnitude (length) and a direction. It is important to distinguish between a vector and its components. The vector \(v\) is an object that is completely independent of any coordinate system.

To describe a vector we introduce a basis. In a coordinate system \(x = (x^1,x^2,\ldots ,x^n)\) we denote the basis vectors by \(e_i\). Any vector \(v\) can then be written as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} v = v^i e_i , \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where the \(v^i\) are the components of \(v\). The components depend on the coordinate system. On the other hand, the vector as a geometrical object it does not depend on the coordinate system, only its components do. Put otherwise, coordinates are simply labels assigned to points in space; changing coordinates should not alter geometric properties.

In the same spirit, coordinate transformations cannot change the meaning of geometric operations such as inner products or directional derivatives. For instance, the dot product of two vectors or the directional derivative of a function produces a scalar. Scalars are coordinate-independent, meaning their value at a point does not depend on the coordinates assigned to that point. Consider two vectors \(u\) and \(v\). In a coordinate system the dot product is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u \cdot v &= (u^i e_i) \cdot (v^j e_j) \\ &= u^i v^j (e_i \cdot e_j). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Now suppose we change coordinates from \(x\) to \(x'\), with new basis vectors \(e'_i\). The same vectors can be written as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u = u'^i e'_i , \qquad v = v'^j e'_j . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The dot product in the new coordinates is
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u \cdot v &= (u'^i e'_i) \cdot (v'^j e'_j) \\ &= u'^i v'^j (e'_i \cdot e'_j). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Although the components and basis vectors have changed, the numerical value of the dot product is a scalar, so it should be the same:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u \cdot v &= u^i v^j (e_i \cdot e_j) \\ &= u'^i v'^j (e'_i \cdot e'_j). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Euclidean Metric

In three-dimensional Euclidean space, a vector \(v\) can be described by three numbers \((v_x, v_y, v_z)\). In index notation, we write

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} v^1 = v_x, \quad v^2 = v_y, \quad v^3 = v_z . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
In a Cartesian coordinate system, the basis vectors are orthonormal:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} e_i \cdot e_j = \delta _{ij} = \begin {cases} 1 & i=j \\ 0 & i \neq j \end {cases} \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The metric is
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} g_{ij} = \delta _{ij}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The dot product becomes
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u \cdot v = \sum _{i,j} g_{ij} u^i v^j . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
In Cartesian coordinates, \(v_i = v^i\), and we can write
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} u \cdot v = g_{ij} u^i v^j = g^{ij} u_i v_j = u^i v_i = u^1v^1+u^2v^2+ u^3v^3 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This is the Einstein summation convention: repeated indices (one up, one down) imply summation.
The Minkowski Metric

In special relativity, the quantity \(g_{\mu \nu } a^{\mu } a^{\nu }\) is invariant. Here \(a^{\mu } = (a^0, a^1, a^2, a^3)\) is a four-vector. The metric tensor is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} g_{\mu \nu } = \text {diag}(1,-1,-1,-1). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The corresponding covariant vector is:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} a_\mu = g_{\mu \nu } a^\nu = (a^0, -a^1, -a^2, -a^3). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The product of two four-vectors can be written as:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} a^{\mu } b_{\mu } = a_{\mu } b^{\mu } = g_{\mu \nu } a^{\mu } b^{\nu } = g^{\mu \nu } a_{\mu } b_{\nu } = a \cdot b = a^0 b^0 - a^1 b^1- a^2 b^2 - a^3 b^3. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

For example

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (p_a^\mu p_b^\nu )(p_{c\,\mu } p_{d\,\ \nu }) &= (p_a^\mu p_b^\nu ) (g_{\mu \rho } p_c^\rho \, g_{\nu \sigma } p_d^\sigma ) \\ &= (p_a^\mu g_{\mu \rho } p_c^\rho ) (p_b^\nu g_{\nu \sigma } p_d^\sigma ) \\ &= (p_a \cdot p_c) (p_b \cdot p_d) \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Be mindful of the following mistakes:

  • Using a Euclidean sum:

    \begin{equation} \begin {aligned} p_a^0 p_c^0 + \vec {p}_a \cdot \vec {p}_c \end {aligned} \end{equation}
    is not Lorentz invariant.

  • Pairing wrong indices:

    \begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (p_a \cdot p_b)(p_c \cdot p_d) \end {aligned} \end{equation}
    gives a scalar with a different physical meaning. In particle physics, indices often track the flow of momentum along lines in a Feynman diagram or other conserved quantities. Therefore, pairing the wrong vectors does not correspond to the intended physical process.

A.3 The Levi-Civita Symbol

3D Case

The value of \(\epsilon _{ijk}\) is determined by the permutation of indices \(\{i,j,k\}\) from \(\{1,2,3\}\).

  • Base value: \(\epsilon _{123} = +1\)

  • Even/Odd permutation rule:

    • Even permutation of \((1,2,3)\) gives \(+1\)

    • Odd permutation of \((1,2,3)\) gives \(-1\)

  • Repeated indices: \(\epsilon _{112} = 0\)

The \(i\)-th component of the cross product is

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} w_i = \epsilon _{ijk} u^j v^k \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Leading to
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} w_1 &= \epsilon _{123} u^2 v^3 + \epsilon _{132} u^3 v^2 = u^2 v^3 + (-1) u^3 v^2 = u^2 v^3 - u^3 v^2, \\ w_2 &= \epsilon _{213} u^1 v^3 + \epsilon _{231} u^3 v^1 \ = (-1) u^1 v^3 + (+1) u^3 v^1 \ = -(u^1 v^3 - u^3 v^1), \\ w_3 &= \epsilon _{312} u^1 v^2 + \epsilon _{321} u^2 v^1 = (+1) u^1 v^2 + (-1) u^2 v^1 = u^1 v^2 - u^2 v^1. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
You can compare the above with the determinant formula you might be more familiar with:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathbf {u} \times \mathbf {v} &= \begin {vmatrix} \mathbf {i} & \mathbf {j} & \mathbf {k} \\ u_x & u_y & u_z \\ v_x & v_y & v_z \end {vmatrix} = (u_y v_z - u_z v_y) \mathbf {i} - (u_x v_z - u_z v_x) \mathbf {j} + (u_x v_y - u_y v_x) \mathbf {k}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
4D Case

In 4D, the indices run from \(\mu = 0\) to \(3\), and we define \(\epsilon _{\mu \nu \rho \sigma }\) with the same rules as in 3D:

  • Base value: \(\epsilon _{0123} = +1\)

  • Even permutations give \(+1\), odd permutations give \(-1\)

  • Repeated indices vanish: \(\epsilon _{0012} = 0\)

The only thing that differs is that indices are raised with the Minkowski metric:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \epsilon ^{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } = g^{\mu \alpha } g^{\nu \beta } g^{\lambda \gamma } g^{\sigma \delta } \epsilon _{\alpha \beta \gamma \delta } \end {aligned} \end{equation}
In particular:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \epsilon ^{0123} &= (1)(-1)(-1)(-1) \epsilon _{0123} = -1 \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Consider

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{\lambda \sigma }_{\kappa \tau } = \epsilon ^{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } \epsilon _{\mu \nu \kappa \tau } \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Non-zero terms require \(\{\lambda ,\sigma \} = \{\kappa ,\tau \}\) otherwise one of the indices that differ would coincide with one of the \(\{\mu ,\nu \}\) and the product would vanish. As an example we can look at the case: \(\lambda = 2\), \(\sigma = 3\)
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{23}_{\kappa \tau } &= \epsilon ^{0123} \epsilon _{01\kappa \tau } + \epsilon ^{1023} \epsilon _{10\kappa \tau } \end {aligned} \end{equation}
If \(\kappa = 2\), \(\tau = 3\)
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{23}_{23} &= \epsilon ^{0123} \epsilon _{0123} + \epsilon ^{1023} \epsilon _{1023} \\ &= (-1)(+1) + (+1)(-1) \\ &= -2 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
If \(\kappa = 3\), \(\tau = 2\)
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{23}_{32} &= \epsilon ^{0123} \epsilon _{0132} + \epsilon ^{1023} \epsilon _{1032} \\ &= (-1)(-1) + (+1)(+1) \\ &= +2 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
If \(\{\kappa ,\tau \} \neq \{\lambda ,\sigma \}\), at least one Levi-Civita symbol contains repeated indices, hence
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{\lambda \sigma }_{\kappa \tau } = 0 \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The above can be repeated for the other cases \(\{\lambda , \sigma \} = \{0,1\}, \{0,2\} ,\{0,3\}, \{1,2\}, \{1,3\} \{2,3\}\), leading to the same result that can be written in terms of Kronecker deltas:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} C^{\lambda \sigma }_{\kappa \tau } = -2 \left ( \delta ^\lambda _{\ \kappa } \delta ^\sigma _{\ \tau } - \delta ^\lambda _{\ \tau } \delta ^\sigma _{\ \kappa } \right ) \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Meaning: \(-2\) when \(\lambda =\kappa \), \(\sigma =\tau \); \(+2\) when \(\lambda =\tau \), \(\sigma =\kappa \); \(0\) otherwise.

The other case we encounter in the Feynman diagram calculations is having a tensor like

\begin{equation} S^{\mu \nu } = p_1^\mu p_2^\nu + p_1^\nu p_2^\mu - g^{\mu \nu } (p_1 \cdot p_2). \end{equation}
multiplied with the Levi-Civita symbol.

The tensor \(S^{\mu \nu }\) is symmetric under the exchange of \(\mu \) and \(\nu \) because:

  • The sum \((p_1^\mu p_2^\nu + p_1^\nu p_2^\mu )\) is invariant if you swap \(\mu \leftrightarrow \nu \).

  • The metric \(g^{\mu \nu }\) is symmetric by definition (\(g^{\mu \nu } = g^{\nu \mu }\)).

An antisymmetric tensor contracted with a symmetric tensor vanishes. Let’s show some of the intermediate steps:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &\left [ p_1^\mu p_2^\nu + p_1^\nu p_2^\mu - g^{\mu \nu } (p_1 \cdot p_2) \right ] \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma \\ &= \underbrace {p_1^\mu p_2^\nu \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma }_{\text {Term A}} + \underbrace {p_1^\nu p_2^\mu \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma }_{\text {Term B}} - \underbrace {g^{\mu \nu } (p_1 \cdot p_2) \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma }_{\text {Term C}}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Relabel dummy indices in Term B (\(\mu \leftrightarrow \nu \)):
\begin{equation} \text {Term B} = p_1^\mu p_2^\nu \epsilon _{\nu \mu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma . \end{equation}
Using the antisymmetry \(\epsilon _{\nu \mu \lambda \sigma } = -\epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma }\):
\begin{equation} \text {Term B} = - p_1^\mu p_2^\nu \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma = -\text {Term A}. \end{equation}
For Term C, define
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} X_{\lambda \sigma } &= g^{\mu \nu } \epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma } \\ &= g^{\nu \mu } \epsilon _{\nu \mu \lambda \sigma } \quad \text {(Relabel } \mu \leftrightarrow \nu \text {)} \\ &= g^{\mu \nu } \epsilon _{\nu \mu \lambda \sigma } \quad \text {(Symmetry: } g^{\mu \nu } = g^{\nu \mu } \text {)} \\ &= g^{\mu \nu } (-\epsilon _{\mu \nu \lambda \sigma }) \quad \text {(Antisymmetry: } \epsilon _{\nu \mu \dots } = -\epsilon _{\mu \nu \dots } \text {)} \\ &= -X_{\lambda \sigma }, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
which implies \(X_{\lambda \sigma } = 0\), so Term C vanishes:
\begin{equation} \text {Term C} = (p_1 \cdot p_2) X_{\lambda \sigma } p_3^\lambda p_4^\sigma = 0. \end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \text {Total} &= \text {Term A} + \text {Term B} - \text {Term C} \\ &= \text {Term A} - \text {Term A} - 0 = 0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

\(\nextSection \)

B Decay Rate Derivations

B.1 Decay Rate Formula

Let’s consider a decay process where the initial state is a single particle with four-momentum \(p\) and mass \(M\), and the final state is given by \(n\) particles with four-momenta \(p_i\) and masses \(m_i\), \(i=1, \dots ,n\). For brevity, we define \(T_{fi}\) as:

\begin{equation} W_{fi} = \frac {1}{(2E_{p} V)^{1/2}} \prod _{i=1}^{n} \frac {1}{(2E_{p_i} V)^{1/2}} \mathcal {M}_{fi}. \end{equation}
So we can write the corresponding S-matrix element:
\begin{equation} \label {eq:SMatrix_M} \mathcal {S}_{fi} = \delta _{fi} + i (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)}(P_f - P_i) W_{fi}. \end{equation}

Step 1: Square Modulus The term \(\delta _{fi}\) is 0 since the initial and final states are different. Quantum mechanics requires that the probability for a process is given by the square of the modulus:

\begin{equation} |i \mathcal {T}_{fi}|^2 = \left | (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)}(P_f - P_i) i W_{fi} \right |^2. \end{equation}
We handle the square of the delta function by writing one of the terms as an integral over a large \(V\) and \(T\):
\begin{equation} (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)}(P_f - P_i) = \int _{V,T} e^{i (P_f-P_i) \cdot x} d^4x = \int _{V,T} d^4x = VT, \end{equation}
where the condition \((P_f-P_i)=0\) is enforced by the other delta function term. Therefore:
\begin{equation} \left | (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)}(P_f - P_i) i W_{fi} \right |^2 = (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)}(P_f - P_i) VT |W_{fi}|^2. \end{equation}

Step 2: Sum over all final states To get the correct probability we need to sum over the possible values of the final particles’ momenta. In the large volume limit, we can write:

\begin{equation} \sum _{\mathbf {p}_i} \rightarrow \frac {V}{(2\pi )^3} \int d^3 \mathbf {p}_i. \end{equation}

In order to understand this point in a bit more “physical” manner, notice that in classical mechanics we use momentum and position phase-space. Classical energy can be connected to quantum mechanics via the number of quantum states per area of phase space energy. The usual integration measure is

\begin{equation} \frac {d^3 \boldsymbol {x}_i \, d^3 \boldsymbol {p}_i}{h^3} \end{equation}
where \(h^3\) is the volume of the cells of phase space.

This can be interpreted as follows. Phase space is not infinitely continuous but is divided into discrete cells. A cell of volume \(h^3\) corresponds to exactly one quantum state, so the density of states in phase space is \(h^{-3}\). Since \(h = 2\pi \hbar \) and we work in units where \(\hbar = 1\), the phase-space measure becomes

\begin{equation} \frac {d^3\mathbf {x}_i\, d^3\mathbf {p}_i}{(2\pi )^3}. \end{equation}
We consider momentum eigenstates, so the result depends on momentum but not on position. Consequently, integrating over \(d^3\mathbf {x}_i\) simply yields the spatial volume \(V\).

Step 3 Bring things together: Therefore, the probability \(dw\) for having in the final state the i\(^\mathrm {th}\) particle with momentum between \(p_i\) and \(p_i+dp_i\) is

\begin{equation} dw = (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)} (P_i - P_f)VT |W_{fi}|^2 \prod _{i=1}^{n} \frac {Vd^3\boldsymbol {p}_i}{(2\pi )^3}. \end{equation}
We are more interested in the decay probability per unit time, so we divide by T
\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi}= (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)} (P_i - P_f) V |W_{fi}|^2 \prod _{i=1}^{n} \frac {Vd^3\boldsymbol {p}_i}{(2\pi )^3}. \end{equation}
Replacing \(W_{fi}\) with the normalized \(\mathcal {M}_{fi}\) gets rid of the volume factors,
\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi} = (2\pi )^4 \delta ^{(4)} (P_i - P_f) \frac {1}{2E_p}| \mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2 \prod _{i=1}^{n} \frac {d^3\boldsymbol {p}_i}{(2\pi )^3 (2E_i)}. \end{equation}

What about all these factors of \(2\pi \) we encountered? You can keep track of them using the following rule: Every \(\delta \) gets (\(2\pi \)); every \(d\) gets \(1/2\pi \).

B.2 Two-Body Decays

Consider the two-body decay \(a \to 1+2\). In the centre-of-mass frame, the decaying particle is at rest, \(E_a = m_a\) and \(\mathbf {p}_a = 0\), and the two daughter particles are produced back-to-back with three- momenta \(\mathbf {p}^*\) and \(-\mathbf {p}^*\). In this frame, the decay rate is given by

\begin{equation} \label {eq:two_body_initial} \Gamma _{fi} = \frac {1}{8\pi ^2 m_a} \int \frac {|\mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2}{2 E_1 2 E_2} \delta (m_a - E_1 - E_2) \delta ^{(3)}(\mathbf {p}_1 + \mathbf {p}_2) \, d^3 \mathbf {p}_1 \, d^3 \mathbf {p}_2. \end{equation}

The \(\delta ^{(3)}(\mathbf {p}_1 + \mathbf {p}_2)\) term enforces momentum conservation, so that integrating over \(d^3 \mathbf {p}_2\) gives \(\mathbf {p}_2 = -\mathbf {p}_1\), reducing the decay rate to

\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi} = \frac {1}{8\pi ^2 m_a} \int \frac {|\mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2}{4 E_1 E_2} \delta (m_a - E_1 - E_2) \, d^3 \mathbf {p}_1, \end{equation}
where now \(E_2 = \sqrt {m_2^2 + \mathbf {p}_1^2}\).

Using spherical polar coordinates \(d^3 \mathbf {p}_1 = p_1^2 dp_1 \, d\Omega ^*\), we can write

\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi} = \frac {1}{8\pi ^2 m_a} \int \frac {|\mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2 p_1^2}{4 E_1 E_2} \delta \big (m_a - E_1 - E_2\big ) \, dp_1 \, d\Omega ^*. \end{equation}
We now define
\begin{align} g(p_1) &= \frac {p_1^2}{4 E_1 E_2}, \\ f(p_1) &= m_a - E_1 - E_2 = m_a - \sqrt {m_1^2 + p_1^2} - \sqrt {m_2^2 + p_1^2}. \end{align}

Then the decay rate can be written as

\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi} = \frac {1}{8\pi ^2 m_a} \int |\mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2 g(p_1) \, \delta (f(p_1)) \, dp_1 \, d\Omega ^*. \end{equation}
The delta function \(\delta (f(p_1))\) is only non-zero for \(p_1 = p^*\), where \(p^*\) is the solution of \(f(p^*)=0\). Using the following property of the Dirac delta function,
\begin{equation} \int g(p_1) \, \delta (f(p_1)) \, dp_1 = \frac {g(p^*)}{|df/dp_1|_{p_1 = p^*}}, \end{equation}
we calculate the derivative
\begin{equation} \frac {df}{dp_1} = - \frac {p_1}{E_1} - \frac {p_1}{E_2} = -p_1 \left ( \frac {1}{E_1} + \frac {1}{E_2} \right ), \end{equation}
so that
\begin{equation} \left | \frac {df}{dp_1} \right |^{-1} = \frac {1}{p_1} \frac {E_1 E_2}{E_1 + E_2}. \end{equation}
Combining with \(g(p_1)\) gives
\begin{equation} g(p^*) \left | \frac {df}{dp_1} \right |^{-1} = \frac {p^{*2}}{4 E_1 E_2} \cdot \frac {E_1 E_2}{p^*(E_1 + E_2)} = \frac {p^*}{4(E_1 + E_2)} = \frac {p^*}{4 m_a}, \end{equation}
where we used \(E_1 + E_2 = m_a\). Thus the differential decay rate is
\begin{equation} \Gamma _{fi} = \frac {p^*}{32 \pi ^2 m_a^2} |\mathcal {M}_{fi}|^2 d\Omega ^*. \end{equation}

\(\nextSection \)

C Maxwell Equations

C.1 Covariant Formulation of Maxwell’s Equations

Recall that Maxwell’s equations can be written using Heaviside-Lorentz natural units in the form:

\begin{equation} \label {eq:Maxwell} \begin {aligned} &\boldsymbol {\nabla } \cdot \boldsymbol {E} = \rho ,\\ &\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \boldsymbol {B} - \frac {\partial \boldsymbol {E}}{\partial t} = \boldsymbol {J},\\ &\boldsymbol {\nabla } \cdot \boldsymbol {B} = 0,\\ &\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \boldsymbol {E} + \frac {\partial \boldsymbol {B}}{\partial t} = 0, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where \(\rho \) is the local charge density and \(\boldsymbol {J}\) is the current density.

These equations can be rewritten in a manifestly Lorentz invariant form, using the following procedure. We take \(\frac {\partial }{\partial t}\) of the first equation and the \(\boldsymbol {\nabla }\) of the second equation and add them together. Since the divergence of a curl is zero (\(\boldsymbol {\nabla } \cdot (\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \boldsymbol {A})=0\)) and we can exchange the order of \(\frac {\partial }{\partial t}\) and \(\boldsymbol {\nabla }\) (Schwarz’s theorem), we obtain the local conservation of charge:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {\partial \rho }{\partial t} + \boldsymbol {\nabla } \cdot \boldsymbol {J} = 0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The \(\rho \) and \(\boldsymbol {J}\) constitute a four-vector \(J^\mu = (\rho ,\boldsymbol {J})\).

The third equation implies that we can write \(\boldsymbol {B}\) as the curl of a vector potential \(\boldsymbol {A}\)

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \boldsymbol {B} = \boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \boldsymbol {A}. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
With this, the fourth equation becomes
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \left (\boldsymbol {E} + \frac {\partial \boldsymbol {A}}{\partial t}\right ) = 0, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
meaning that (since the curl of a gradient is zero, \(\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times (\boldsymbol {\nabla } A^0)=0\)) we can write
\begin{equation} \label {eq:Maxwell_E} \begin {aligned} \boldsymbol {E} = -\frac {\partial \boldsymbol {A}} {\partial t} - \boldsymbol {\nabla } A^0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The scalar potential \(A^0\) represents the energy per unit charge locally available, while the vector potential \(\boldsymbol {A}\) represents the momentum per unit charge locally available for exchange between charged matter and the field.

We can assemble the potentials in a four-vector \(A^\mu = (A^0, \boldsymbol {A})\) and use that to define the antisymmetric field strength tensor

\begin{equation} \label {eq:EMTensor} \begin {aligned} F^{\mu \nu } = \partial ^\mu A^\nu - \partial ^\nu A^\mu . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
With a bit of patience we can work out the components of \(F^{\mu \nu }\), for example
\begin{equation} F^{10} = \partial ^1 A^0 - \partial ^0 A^1 = -\frac {\partial A^0}{\partial x^1} - \frac {\partial A^1}{\partial x^0} = E_x, \end{equation}
where we used the definition of \(\partial ^\mu \) from Section 1.5 and equation C.5. The end result is
\begin{equation} F^{\mu \nu } = \left (\begin {array}{cccc} 0 & -E_x & -E_y & -E_z \\ E_x & 0 & -B_z & B_y \\ E_y & B_z & 0 & -B_x \\ E_z & -B_y & B_x & 0 \end {array}\right ). \end{equation}
We can also obtain
\begin{equation} F_{\mu \nu } = \left (\begin {array}{cccc} 0 & E_x & E_y & E_z \\ - E_x & 0 & -B_z & B_y \\ - E_y & B_z & 0 & -B_x \\ - E_z & -B_y & B_x & 0 \end {array}\right ). \end{equation}
Finally, we define the dual of the field strength tensor
\begin{equation} \tilde {F}_{\mu \nu } = \frac {1}{2} \epsilon _{\mu \nu \rho \sigma } F^{\rho \sigma } = \left (\begin {array}{cccc} 0 & B_x & B_y & B_z \\ - B_x & 0 & E_z & -E_y \\ - B_y & -E_z & 0 & E_x \\ - B_z & E_y & -E_x & 0 \end {array}\right ). \end{equation}

The first two of Maxwell’s equations in C.1 can then be written as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \partial _\mu F^{\mu \nu } = \partial _\mu \partial ^\mu A^{\nu } - \partial ^{\nu }(\partial _\mu A^{\mu }) = J^\nu , \end {aligned} \end{equation}
and the last two as
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \partial _\mu \tilde {F}^{\mu \nu } = 0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

C.2 Aharonov–Bohm Effect

The Aharonov–Bohm effect is about examining the two-slit electron experiment in the presence of a solenoid; see figure C.1. With no current in the solenoid, electrons form an interference pattern on the screen. As soon as current flows through the solenoid, the diffraction pattern changes.

The probability amplitude \(\psi _1\) for an electron to follow path 1 and the amplitude \(\psi _2\) for path 2 are modified:

\begin{equation} \psi _1=\psi _{01}\exp \!\left (-\frac {i q}{\hbar } S_1\right ), \quad \psi _2=\psi _{02}\exp \!\left (-\frac {i q}{\hbar } S_2\right ) \end{equation}
where \(\psi _{01}\) and \(\psi _{02}\) are the amplitudes without the current, \(q\) is the electron charge, and \(S_1\), \(S_2\) are extra phases:
\begin{equation} S_1=\int _{\text {path 1}} \mathbf {A}\cdot d\mathbf {r}, \quad S_2=\int _{\text {path 2}} \mathbf {A}\cdot d\mathbf {r} \end{equation}

The modification of the phase on the screen is then:

\begin{equation} \frac {q}{\hbar }(S_1-S_2) = \frac {q}{\hbar } \left ( \int _{\text {path 1}} A\cdot dr - \int _{\text {path 2}} A\cdot dr \right ) = \frac {q}{\hbar } \oint _{\text {closed path}} A\cdot dr \end{equation}

For an infinitely long thin solenoid, there is no magnetic field \(B\) outside. The experimentally observed modification of the interference pattern is therefore due to the vector potential \(A\), present outside the solenoid.

The vector potential \(\mathbf {A}\) is related to the space-dependent phase and the scalar potential \(\phi \) to the time-dependent phase. From the phase of the probability amplitude, one can obtain the 4-vector potential, and vice versa.

Classically, \(\mathbf {B}\) is considered the fundamental quantity and \(A\) is seen more as a mathematical curiosity. This is not true at the quantum level, where \(\mathbf {A}\) can have an effect in the absence of \(\mathbf {B}\). The Standard Model is formulated in terms of gauge fields (like \(A^\mu \)) rather than force fields (like \(\mathbf {B}\) and \(\mathbf {E}\)).

C.3 Maxwell Lagrangian

The Lagrangian of the free electromagnetic field is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = - \frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu }. \end{equation}
We write
\begin{equation} F^{\alpha \beta } = g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } F_{\rho \sigma }. \end{equation}
Notice I introduced new indices as the \(\mu \), \(\nu \) will be taken by the equation of motion. Therefore,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} F_{\alpha \beta } F^{\alpha \beta } &= g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } F_{\alpha \beta } F_{\rho \sigma } \\ &= g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } (\partial _\alpha A_\beta - \partial _\beta A_\alpha ) (\partial _\rho A_\sigma - \partial _\sigma A_\rho ) \\ &= g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } \Big ( \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\rho A_\sigma - \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\sigma A_\rho - \partial _\beta A_\alpha \partial _\rho A_\sigma + \partial _\beta A_\alpha \partial _\sigma A_\rho \Big ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Notice that

\begin{equation*} \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\rho A_\sigma - \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\sigma A_\rho \end{equation*}
and
\begin{equation*} -\partial _\beta A_\alpha \partial _\rho A_\sigma + \partial _\beta A_\alpha \partial _\sigma A_\rho \end{equation*}
are symmetric under exchange of indices \(\alpha \leftrightarrow \beta \) and \(\sigma \leftrightarrow \rho \). Therefore,
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu A_\nu )} &= -\frac {1}{2} g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } \frac {\partial }{\partial (\partial _\mu A_\nu )} \Big ( \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\rho A_\sigma - \partial _\alpha A_\beta \partial _\sigma A_\rho \Big ) \\ &= -\frac {1}{2} g^{\alpha \rho } g^{\beta \sigma } \Big ( {\delta _\alpha }^\mu {\delta _\beta }^\nu \partial _\rho A_\sigma + {\delta _\rho }^\mu {\delta _\sigma }^\nu \partial _\alpha A_\beta - {\delta _\alpha }^\mu {\delta _\beta }^\nu \partial _\sigma A_\rho - {\delta _\sigma }^\mu {\delta _\rho }^\nu \partial _\alpha A_\beta \Big ) \\ &= - \partial ^\mu A^\nu + \partial ^\nu A^\mu = - F^{\mu \nu }, \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where we used
\begin{equation} \frac {\partial (\partial _\lambda A_\rho )} {\partial (\partial _\mu A_\nu )} = {\delta _\lambda }^\mu {\delta _\rho }^\nu \end{equation}
since \(\partial _\mu A_\nu \) are 16 independent components. Substituting into
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \left ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu A_\nu )} \right ) - \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial A_\nu } = 0 \end{equation}
gives
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu F^{\mu \nu } = 0. \end{equation}

C.4 Proca Lagrangian

An interesting extension of Maxwell’s equations is the inclusion of a mass term. The Lagrangian is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = - \frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu } + \frac {1}{2} m^2 A_\mu A^\mu . \end{equation}
The additional term gives a mass \(m\) to the vector field \(A^\mu \). Applying the Euler-Lagrange equations yields
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu F^{\mu \nu } + m^2 A^\nu = 0. \end{equation}

Unlike the Maxwell case, the Proca Lagrangian is not invariant under gauge transformations. The mass term breaks gauge invariance and leads to three physical degrees of freedom, corresponding to a massive spin-1 particle.

\(\nextSection \)

D Dirac Equation And Spin

D.1 Dirac Hamiltonian and Spin

For the free-particle Dirac Hamiltonian,

\begin{equation} \hat {H}_D = \boldsymbol {\alpha } \cdot \hat {\mathbf {p}} + \beta m, \end{equation}
the commutation relation with the orbital angular momentum operator \(\hat {\mathbf {L}} = \hat {\mathbf {r}} \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}\) is given by:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {L}}\right ] &= [\boldsymbol {\alpha } \cdot \hat {\mathbf {p}} + \beta m, \hat {\mathbf {r}} \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}] \\ &= [\boldsymbol {\alpha } \cdot \hat {\mathbf {p}}, \hat {\mathbf {r}} \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Evaluating the \(x\)-component, \(\hat {L}_x = \hat {y} \hat {p}_z - \hat {z} \hat {p}_y\):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left [ \hat {H}_D, \hat {L}_x \right ] &= [\alpha _x \hat {p}_x + \alpha _y \hat {p}_y + \alpha _z \hat {p}_z, \hat {y} \hat {p}_z - \hat {z} \hat {p}_y] \\ &= \alpha _y [\hat {p}_y, \hat {y}] \hat {p}_z - \alpha _z [\hat {p}_z, \hat {z}] \hat {p}_y. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the canonical commutator \([\hat {x}_i, \hat {p}_j] = i \delta _{ij}\) (where \(\hbar =1\)):
\begin{equation} [\hat {H}_D, \hat {L}_x] = -i (\alpha _y \hat {p}_z - \alpha _z \hat {p}_y) = -i (\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}})_x. \end{equation}
Generalizing to all components yields:
\begin{equation} [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {L}}] = - i (\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}). \end{equation}
Since \([\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {L}}] \neq 0\), the orbital angular momentum \(\hat {\mathbf {L}}\) is not a conserved quantity for a Dirac particle.

Now, consider the spin operator \(\hat {\mathbf {S}} = \frac {1}{2} \hat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma }}\), where \(\hat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma }}\) is the \(4 \times 4\) version of the Pauli matrices:

\begin{equation} \hat {\boldsymbol {\Sigma }} \equiv \left ( \begin {array}{cc} \boldsymbol {\sigma } & 0 \\ 0 & \boldsymbol {\sigma } \end {array} \right ). \end{equation}
The commutator of the Dirac Hamiltonian with the \(x\)-component of spin is:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\Sigma }_x \right ] &= [\boldsymbol {\alpha } \cdot \hat {\mathbf {p}}, \hat {\Sigma }_x] \\ &= \hat {p}_x [\alpha _x, \hat {\Sigma }_x] + \hat {p}_y [\alpha _y, \hat {\Sigma }_x] + \hat {p}_z [\alpha _z, \hat {\Sigma }_x]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the relation \([\sigma _i, \sigma _j] = 2i\epsilon _{ijk}\sigma _k\), we find:
\begin{equation} [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\Sigma }_x] = -2i \hat {p}_y \alpha _z + 2i \hat {p}_z \alpha _y = 2i (\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}})_x. \end{equation}
For the full vector operator \(\hat {\mathbf {S}}\):
\begin{equation} [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {S}}] = i (\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}). \end{equation}
By combining orbital and spin angular momentum, \(\hat {\mathbf {J}} = \hat {\mathbf {L}} + \hat {\mathbf {S}}\), we see that:
\begin{equation} [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {J}}] = [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {L}}] + [\hat {H}_D, \hat {\mathbf {S}}] = -i(\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}) + i(\boldsymbol {\alpha } \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}) = 0. \end{equation}
Thus, total angular momentum \(\hat {\mathbf {J}}\) is conserved, identifying \(\hat {\mathbf {S}}\) as the intrinsic spin.

D.2 Pauli Identity

Starting from the multiplication rule of Pauli matrices:

\begin{equation} \sigma _i \sigma _j = \delta _{ij} I + i \sum _{k} \epsilon _{ijk} \sigma _k, \end{equation}
we have
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot \mathbf {a})(\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot \mathbf {b}) &= \sum _{i,j} a_i b_j \sigma _i \sigma _j\\ &= \sum _{i,j} a_i b_j (\delta _{ij} I + i \sum _k \epsilon _{ijk} \sigma _k)\\ &= \sum _i a_i b_i I + i \sum _{i,j,k} a_i b_j \epsilon _{ijk} \sigma _k. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Rewriting the sums in terms of dot and cross products gives
\begin{equation} \sum _i a_i b_i I = (\mathbf {a} \cdot \mathbf {b}) I, \quad \sum _{i,j,k} a_i b_j \epsilon _{ijk} \sigma _k = \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\mathbf {a} \times \mathbf {b}), \end{equation}
which leads to the Pauli identity:
\begin{equation} (\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot \mathbf {a})(\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot \mathbf {b}) = (\mathbf {a} \cdot \mathbf {b}) I + i \, \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\mathbf {a} \times \mathbf {b}). \end{equation}

D.3 Magnetic Moment of the Dirac Particle

Starting from the Dirac equation with the minimal substitution \( \hat {\mathbf {p}} = \hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A} \):

\begin{equation} \left [ \boldsymbol {\alpha } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) + \beta m \right ] \psi = (E - Qe \phi ) \psi , \end{equation}
and splitting the wavefunction into two components gives
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (E - m - Qe\, \phi ) \psi _A &= \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) \psi _B,\\ (E + m - Qe\, \phi ) \psi _B &= \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) \psi _A. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
In the non-relativistic limit (\(E \approx m\) and \(Qe\phi \ll m\)):
\begin{equation} \psi _B \approx \frac {\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A})}{2m} \psi _A, \end{equation}
which substituted back into the equation for \(\psi _A\) yields
\begin{equation} (E - m - Qe \phi ) \psi _A = \frac {\left [ \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) \right ]^2}{2m} \psi _A. \end{equation}
Using the Pauli identity
\begin{equation} [\boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A})]^2 = (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A})^2 + i \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot [(\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) \times (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A})], \end{equation}
and expanding the cross product gives
\begin{equation} (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) \times (\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A}) = -Qe (\hat {\mathbf {p}} \times \mathbf {A} + \mathbf {A} \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}). \end{equation}
Using \(\hat {\mathbf {p}} = -i \boldsymbol {\nabla }\) and the identity \(\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times (\mathbf {A} \psi ) = (\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \mathbf {A}) \psi - \mathbf {A} \times (\boldsymbol {\nabla } \psi )\), the above leads to
\begin{equation} -Qe (\hat {\mathbf {p}} \times \mathbf {A} + \mathbf {A} \times \hat {\mathbf {p}}) = i Qe (\boldsymbol {\nabla } \times \mathbf {A}) = i Qe \mathbf {B}. \end{equation}
Substituting back, the non-relativistic energy equation becomes
\begin{equation} E \psi _A = \left [ m + Qe \phi + \frac {(\hat {\mathbf {p}} - Qe \mathbf {A})^2}{2m} - \frac {Qe}{2m} \boldsymbol {\sigma } \cdot \mathbf {B} \right ] \psi _A, \end{equation}
from which the magnetic moment for an electron (\(Q=-1\)) is identified as
\begin{equation} \boldsymbol {\mu } = \frac {Qe}{2m} \boldsymbol {\sigma }= -\frac {e}{2m} \boldsymbol {\sigma } = -g\frac {e}{2m} \mathbf {S}. \end{equation}
Since
\begin{equation} \mathbf {S} = \frac {1}{2} \boldsymbol {\sigma }, \end{equation}
the Dirac theory predicts a value \(g=2\). While the Dirac equation predicts \(g=2\), radiative corrections-higher-order Feynman diagrams-account for the small but measurable deviation known as the anomalous magnetic moment \(g-2\).

\(\nextSection \)

E Gamma Matrix Identities and Trace Techniques

The Dirac \(\gamma \)-matrices satisfy the fundamental anti-commutation relation:

\begin{equation} \{\gamma ^\mu , \gamma ^\nu \} = \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu + \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu = 2 g^{\mu \nu } I. \end{equation}

E.1 Contraction Identities

When evaluating contractions, the objective is to move the leading \(\gamma ^\mu \) to the right until it is adjacent to its contraction partner \(\gamma _\mu \). Each “jump over” generates a metric tensor term and a sign change.

Contraction of two matrices In 4 dimensions \(g_{\mu \nu }g^{\mu \nu } = 4\).

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} g_{\mu \nu }\{\gamma ^\mu , \gamma ^\nu \} = g_{\mu \nu }(\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu + \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu ) &= 2 g_{\mu \nu } g^{\mu \nu } \\ 2 \gamma ^\mu \gamma _\mu &= 2 \cdot 4 \\ \gamma ^\mu \gamma _\mu &= 4. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Contraction of three matrices To evaluate \(\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma _\mu \), we move \(\gamma ^\mu \) over \(\gamma ^\nu \):

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma _\mu &= (\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu ) \gamma _\mu \\ &= \big (2 g^{\mu \nu } - \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \big ) \gamma _\mu \end {aligned} \end{equation}
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &= 2 g^{\mu \nu } \gamma _\mu - \gamma ^\nu (\gamma ^\mu \gamma _\mu ) \\ &= 2 \gamma ^\nu - 4 \gamma ^\nu = -2 \gamma ^\nu . \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Contraction of four matrices For longer chains, we repeatedly apply the “jump over” method. Move the leading \(\gamma ^\mu \) to the right over \(\gamma ^\nu \) and apply the 3-matrix contraction result:
