\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu &= (\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu ) \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu \\ &= \big (2 g^{\mu \nu } - \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \big ) \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} &= 2 g^{\mu \nu } \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu - \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu \\ &= 2 \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\nu - \gamma ^\nu (\underbrace {\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma _\mu }_{\text {3-matrix}}) \\ &= 2 \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\nu - \gamma ^\nu (-2 \gamma ^\sigma ) \\ &= 2 \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\nu + 2 \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\sigma \\ &= 2 \{\gamma ^\sigma , \gamma ^\nu \} = 2 (2 g^{\sigma \nu }) = 4 g^{\nu \sigma }. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

E.2 Trace Theorems

Trace evaluations rely on the cyclic property \(\mathrm {Tr}[ABC] = \mathrm {Tr}[BCA]\).

Trace of two matrices By symmetry and linearity:

\begin{equation} \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu ] = \frac {1}{2} \mathrm {Tr}[\{\gamma ^\mu , \gamma ^\nu \}] = \frac {1}{2} \mathrm {Tr}[2 g^{\mu \nu } \mathbb {I}] = g^{\mu \nu } \mathrm {Tr}[\mathbb {I}] = 4 g^{\mu \nu }. \end{equation}

Trace of odd number of matrices Insert \(\gamma ^5 \gamma ^5 = \mathbb {I}\) and move the leftmost \(\gamma ^5\) across each matrix:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\rho ] &= \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^5 \gamma ^5 \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\rho ] \\ &= (-1)^3 \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^5 \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\rho \gamma ^5] \quad \text {(one minus per gamma)} \\ &= - \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\rho ] \\ \implies \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\rho ] &= 0. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

Trace of four matrices Move \(\gamma ^\mu \) step by step to the right using anticommutators:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ] &= \mathrm {Tr}[(2 g^{\mu \nu } - \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu ) \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ] \\ &= 2 g^{\mu \nu } \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ] - \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ] &= \mathrm {Tr}[(2 g^{\mu \sigma } - \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\mu ) \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\beta ] \\ &= 2 g^{\mu \sigma } \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\beta ] - \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\beta ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\beta ] &= 2 g^{\mu \beta } \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\nu ] - \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\beta ] \\ \implies \mathrm {Tr}[\gamma ^\mu \gamma ^\nu \gamma ^\sigma \gamma ^\beta ] &= 4 \left ( g^{\mu \nu } g^{\sigma \beta } - g^{\mu \sigma } g^{\nu \beta } + g^{\mu \beta } g^{\nu \sigma } \right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}

E.3 Casimir’s Trick (Trace Technique)

We begin by defining the quantity \(G\), which typically represents the square of the transition amplitude. Given two \(4 \times 4\) matrices \(\Gamma _1\) and \(\Gamma _2\):

\begin{equation} G \equiv \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _1 u(b) \right ] \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _2 u(b) \right ]^* \end{equation}
Since the term in the second set of brackets is a scalar (\(1 \times 1\) matrix), its complex conjugate its the same as its Hermitian conjugate. Using the identities \((\gamma ^0)^\dagger = \gamma ^0\) and \((\gamma ^0)^2 = 1\):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _2 u(b) \right ]^* &= \left [ u^\dagger (a)\gamma ^0 \Gamma _2 u(b) \right ]^\dagger \\ &= u^\dagger (b)\Gamma _2^\dagger (\gamma ^0)^\dagger u(a) \\ &= u^\dagger (b) (\gamma ^0 \gamma ^0) \Gamma _2^\dagger \gamma ^0 u(a) \\ &= \left ( u^\dagger (b) \gamma ^0 \right ) \left ( \gamma ^0 \Gamma _2^\dagger \gamma ^0 \right ) u(a) \\ &= \bar {u}(b) \bar {\Gamma }_2 u(a), \end {aligned} \end{equation}
where we define \(\bar {\Gamma }_2 \equiv \gamma ^0 \Gamma _2^\dagger \gamma ^0\). Thus:
\begin{equation} G = \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _1 u(b) \right ] \left [ \bar {u}(b) \bar {\Gamma }_2 u(a) \right ]. \end{equation}
Summing over the spin orientations of particle \(b\) using the completeness relation
\begin{equation} \sum _{s} u^{(s)}(p)\bar {u}^{(s)} (p) = \not \!p + m, \end{equation}
we obtain
\begin{equation} \sum _{b \text { spins}} G = \bar {u}(a) \Gamma _1 \left ( \sum _{s_b=1,2} u^{(s_b)}(p_b)\bar {u}^{(s_b)}(p_b) \right ) \bar {\Gamma }_2 u(a) = \bar {u}(a) \left [ \Gamma _1(\not \!p_b + m_b)\bar {\Gamma }_2 \right ] u(a). \end{equation}
Let \(Q = \Gamma _1(\not \!p_b + m_b)\bar {\Gamma }_2\) be a temporary \(4 \times 4\) matrix.

To sum over the spins of particle \(a\), we use the identity that a dot product of a row and column vector is the trace of their outer product:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \left ( \begin {array}{cccc} a_1 & a_2 & a_3 & a_4 \end {array} \right ) \left (\begin {array}{c} b_1 \\ b_2 \\b_3 \\ b_4 \end {array} \right ) &= \mbox {Tr} \big [ \left (\begin {array}{c} b_1 \\ b_2 \\b_3 \\ b_4 \end {array} \right ) \left ( \begin {array}{cccc} a_1 & a_2 & a_3 & a_4 \end {array} \right ) \big ] \\ & = \mbox {Tr} \left ( \begin {array}{cccc} b_1 a_1 & b_1 a_2 & b_1 a_3 & b_1 a_4 \\ b_2 a_1 & b_2 a_2 & b_2 a_3 & b_2 a_4 \\ b_3 a_1 & b_3 a_2 & b_3 a_3 & b_3 a_4 \\ b_4 a_1 & b_4 a_2 & b_4 a_3 & b_4 a_4 \end {array} \right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Or equivalently, in index notation:
\begin{equation} \sum _{i,j} A_{ij} B_{ji} = \sum _i (AB)_{ii} = \mbox {Tr} (AB). \end{equation}
Applying this to our spinor sum by writing out the indices explicitly:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \sum _{\text {all spins}} G &= \sum _{s_a} \sum _{i,j} \bar {u}_i^{(s_a)} Q_{ij} u_j^{(s_a)} \\ &= \sum _{i,j} Q_{ij} \left ( \sum _{s_a} u_j^{(s_a)} \bar {u}_i^{(s_a)} \right ) \\ &= \sum _{i,j} Q_{ij} (\not \!p_a + m_a )_{ji} \\ &= \sum _i \left [ Q (\not \!p_a + m_a ) \right ]_{ii} \\ &= \mbox {Tr} \left [ Q (\not \!p_a + m_a ) \right ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}

The end result is:

\begin{equation} \sum _{\text {all spins}} \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _1 u(b) \right ] \left [ \bar {u}(a)\Gamma _2 u(b) \right ]^* = \mbox {Tr} \left [ \Gamma _1(\not \!p_b + m_b)\bar {\Gamma }_2(\not \!p_a + m_a) \right ]. \end{equation}

\(\nextSection \)

F A Very Short Introduction to Lie Groups

Only a short introduction to some key concepts is provided; interested readers can consult [24, 25].

Definition

A group is a set of elements \(G = \{g_i\}\) together with a rule for multiplying them, with the properties:

  • Closure: If \(g_i\) and \(g_j\) are elements of the group \(G\), then the product \(g_i \cdot g_j\) is also an element of \(G\).

  • Associativity: \(g_i \cdot (g_j \cdot g_k) = (g_i \cdot g_j) \cdot g_k\).

  • Existence of an Identity: There is a unique element \(I = g_1\) of the group such that for all \(g_i\) in \(G\), \(I \cdot g_i = g_i \cdot I = g_i\).

  • Inversion: For each \(g_i\), there is a unique inverse element \((g_i)^{-1}\) satisfying \((g_i)^{-1} \cdot g_i = g_i \cdot (g_i)^{-1} = I\).

Groups may be Abelian or non-Abelian according to whether the result of the binary operation depends upon the order in which the input elements are used: if \(g_i \cdot g_j = g_j \cdot g_i\) the group is Abelian, otherwise it is non-Abelian.

F.1 Representations

In physics, we are interested in the action of group elements on physical states. Thus, we focus more on representations rather than the groups themselves. A representation is the association of group elements with \(N \times N\) matrices acting on \(N\)-dimensional complex vectors, denoted by \(\mathcal {D}\):

\begin{equation} \begin {array}{ccccc} g_1 & \cdot & g_2 & = & g_1 g_2 \\ \downarrow & & \downarrow & & \downarrow \\ \mathcal {D}(g_1) & \cdot & \mathcal {D}(g_2) & = & \mathcal {D}(g_1 g_2) \end {array}. \end{equation}
Here, the \(\cdot \) symbol in the top row denotes the multiplication of two group elements, while in the bottom row it denotes multiplication of two matrices.

If, for an arbitrary element \(a\), the matrix \(\mathcal {D}(a)\) can be diagonalized to the block form

\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(a) = \begin {pmatrix} D_1(a) & 0 & \cdots & 0 \\ 0 & D_2(a) & \cdots & 0 \\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\ 0 & 0 & \cdots & D_k(a) \end {pmatrix}, \label {eq:block_diag} \end{equation}
where each \(D_i(a)\) is a smaller matrix, the representation is said to be reducible, and we write
\begin{equation} \mathcal {D} = D_1 \oplus D_2 \oplus \cdots \oplus D_k. \label {eq:reducible_rep} \end{equation}

To connect the abstract group elements with a representation, consider a simple finite example: the group of permutations of four objects, \(S_4\). For instance, the permutation \((2413)\) acts as

\begin{equation*} 2 \to 4, \quad 4 \to 1, \quad 1 \to 3, \quad 3 \to 2. \end{equation*}
We can represent the four objects by the basis vectors
\begin{equation} v_1 = \begin {pmatrix}1\\0\\0\\0\end {pmatrix}, \quad v_2 = \begin {pmatrix}0\\1\\0\\0\end {pmatrix}, \quad v_3 = \begin {pmatrix}0\\0\\1\\0\end {pmatrix}, \quad v_4 = \begin {pmatrix}0\\0\\0\\1\end {pmatrix}. \end{equation}
A permutation acts by reshuffling these basis vectors. It can be represented by \(4 \times 4\) matrices, \(\mathcal {D}\), of \(S_4\). In this case,
\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(2413) = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 & 1 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}. \end{equation}
The matrix acts on the basis vectors in the same way as the permutation:
\begin{equation*} \mathcal {D}(2413)\, v_2 = v_4, \quad \mathcal {D}(2413)\, v_4 = v_1, \quad \mathcal {D}(2413)\, v_1 = v_3, \quad \mathcal {D}(2413)\, v_3 = v_2. \end{equation*}
Thus, the action of the matrix \(\mathcal {D}(2413)\) on vectors expresses the action of the permutation \((2413)\) on the four objects.

F.2 Lie Groups, Generators and Algebra

Lie groups are groups with an infinite number of elements that can be smoothly parametrized by one or more continuous parameters. Since the identity is an important element of the group, it is useful to parametrize elements such that \(\theta ^a = 0\) corresponds to the identity element. In some neighbourhood of the identity, group elements can be written as \(g(\theta )\), where \(\theta ^a, \, a = 1, \dots , N,\) are real parameters, and

\begin{equation} g(\theta )\big |_{\theta =0} = I. \end{equation}

If we consider a representation of the group, the corresponding linear operators are parametrized in the same way:

\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(\theta )\big |_{\theta =0} = 1. \end{equation}
In a neighbourhood of the identity, we can Taylor expand \(\mathcal {D}(\theta )\). For infinitesimal parameters \(\theta ^a\), we keep only the leading term:
\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(\theta ) = 1 + i\, \theta ^a T^a + \cdots . \end{equation}
Here, a sum over repeated indices is understood. The matrices \(T^a\) are defined by
\begin{equation} T^a = -i \left .\frac {\partial \mathcal {D}(\theta )} {\partial \theta ^a}\right |_{\theta =0}, \end{equation}
and are called the generators of the group.

We can move away from the identity in a fixed direction by exponentiating an infinitesimal transformation. Using the group property, we define finite transformations as

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \mathcal {D}(\theta ) &= \lim _{k \to \infty } \left ( 1 + i\, \frac {\theta ^a}{k} T^a \right )^k = \exp \left (i \theta ^a T^a\right ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This defines a particular parametrization (the exponential parametrization) of the representation. Group elements (at least near the identity) can be written in terms of the generators. This is useful because, unlike “abstract” group elements, the generators form a vector space: they can be added and multiplied by real numbers. Along a single direction in parameter space,
\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(\alpha ) = \exp (i \alpha T^a), \end{equation}
the group multiplication law is simple:
\begin{equation} \mathcal {D}(\alpha )\mathcal {D}(\beta ) = \mathcal {D}(\alpha + \beta ) = \exp (i (\alpha +\beta ) T^a). \end{equation}

However, if we consider two different directions, the situation is more complicated as

\begin{equation} \exp (i \alpha T^a)\, \exp (i \beta T^b) \neq \exp \big (i(\alpha T^a + \beta T^b)\big ). \end{equation}
On the other hand, exponentials are a representation of the group (at least near the identity), so their product must still be expressible as an exponential:
\begin{equation} \exp (i \alpha T^a)\, \exp (i \beta T^b) = \exp \big (i \gamma ^c T^c\big ), \end{equation}
for some parameters \(\gamma ^c\). Because the group is smooth, we can expand both sides in \(\alpha \) and \(\beta \). Using the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff (BCH) formula,
\begin{equation} \exp (i \alpha ^a T^a)\exp (i \beta ^b T^b) = \exp \Big (i \alpha ^a T^a + i \beta ^b T^b - \tfrac {1}{2} \alpha ^a \beta ^b [T^a,T^b] + \cdots \Big ), \end{equation}
where repeated indices \(a,b\) are summed over. Comparing with
\begin{equation} \exp \big (i \gamma ^c T^c\big ) = \exp \Big (i \gamma ^c T^c + \cdots \Big ), \end{equation}
defining
\begin{equation} \gamma ^c = \alpha ^a \beta ^b f^{abc}, \end{equation}
and matching the \(O(\alpha \beta )\) terms gives:
\begin{equation} [T^a,T^b] = i f^{abc} T^c. \label {eq:Lie_algebra} \end{equation}
The parameters \(f^{abc}\) are called structure constants. The commutator above defines the Lie algebra corresponding to the Lie group. For Abelian groups \(f^{abc} = 0\). For non-Abelian groups, in practice we pick a particular set of matrices \(T^a\) as the defining or fundamental representation; this fixes the structure of the algebra.

F.3 Particle Physics Examples

A couple of examples relevant to particle physics can help clarify the above abstract terminology.

U(1) The simplest Lie group is the Abelian \(U(1)\) group. The elements of the \(U(1)\) group are points on the unit circle, which can be labelled by a unit complex number \(e^{i\theta }\). Since complex numbers \(e^{i\theta }\) are \(1\times 1\) unitary matrices, \(U(1)\) is also the group of unitary matrices of dimension \(1\). The representations of \(U(1)\), used in physics, are \(U = e^{i Q \theta }\) with \(Q\) an integer. Here, \(\theta \) labels the group elements, and the charge \(Q\) labels the representation of the group.

SU(N) \(SU(N)\) is the Lie group of \(N\times N\) unitary matrices with determinant 1. We define the representation

\begin{equation*} U = e^{i\theta T}. \end{equation*}
The unitary requirement \(UU^\dagger = 1\) implies
\begin{equation*} U^\dagger U = I \implies e^{-i\theta T^\dagger } e^{i\theta T} = I \implies e^{-i\theta T^\dagger + i\theta T + \frac {1}{2}\theta [T^\dagger ,T] + \cdots } \implies T^\dagger = T, \end{equation*}
where in the third step we used the BCH formula. Therefore, the generators are Hermitian. We also have
\begin{equation*} \det (U) = \det (e^{i\theta T}) = e^{\mathrm {Tr}(i\theta T)} = 1 \end{equation*}
for any \(\theta \), meaning that the generators are traceless.

A general \(N \times N\) complex matrix has \(2N^2\) independent entries, since each matrix has \(N^2\) entries with a real and imaginary part. The condition that the generator matrices are Hermitian removes half of these, since each entry in the matrix is required to be the complex conjugate of another entry. Finally, the condition of being traceless removes another one. This means that we can construct the matrix \(A\) as a linear combination of \(N^2-1\) independent matrices. Therefore, we need \(N^2-1\) generators.

For \(SU(2)\), the three generators for the fundamental Lie algebra representation are the Pauli matrices using the normalization convention:

\begin{equation} t^a = \frac {\sigma ^a}{2}, \quad \mathrm {Tr}(t^a t^b) = \frac 12 \delta ^{ab}, \end{equation}
which is the standard “physics” normalization for SU(N). These satisfy \([t^a , t^b] = i \epsilon ^{abc} t^c\), meaning \(f^{abc} = \epsilon ^{abc}\), where \(\epsilon ^{abc}\) is the Levi-Civita symbol.

F.4 The Adjoint Representation and Gauge Fields

When we introduce gauge fields \(W_\mu ^i\), they are included in the Lagrangian in combination with the generators as \(\sigma ^i W_\mu ^i\). This can be seen as a basis expansion of some object \(W_\mu \) in terms of the basis \(\sigma ^i\):

\begin{equation} W_\mu = W_\mu ^i t^i = W_\mu ^1 t^1 + W_\mu ^2 t^2 + W_\mu ^3 t^3, \end{equation}
The generators \(t^i\) live in the Lie algebra of SU(2) and, consequently-since the Lie algebra defines the vector space-our object \(W_\mu \) lives there, too. Therefore, if we want to know how \(W_\mu \) transforms, we need to know the representation of SU(2) on this vector space.

This representation is called the adjoint representation. Gauge fields (like \(W^+, W^-, W^3\)) are said to live in the adjoint representation of the corresponding group. For SU(2), the Lie algebra is three-dimensional because we have three basis generators; therefore, the adjoint representation is three-dimensional. To find the explicit form of these \(3 \times 3\) matrices, denoted \(T_{adj}\), we use the following relationship:

\begin{equation} (T_{adj}^a)_{bc} = -i f^{abc}. \end{equation}
For SU(2), we have \([t^a, t^b] = i \epsilon ^{abc} t^c\), therefore \(f^{abc} = \epsilon ^{abc}\) and the matrix elements are given by:
\begin{equation} (T_{adj}^a)_{bc} = -i \epsilon ^{abc}. \end{equation}

We can write out the three generators explicitly:

\begin{equation} T_{adj}^1 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & -i \\ 0 & i & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad T_{adj}^2 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & 0 & i \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \\ -i & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}, \quad T_{adj}^3 = \begin {pmatrix} 0 & -i & 0 \\ i & 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 \end {pmatrix}. \end{equation}
These matrices satisfy the same commutation relations as the Pauli matrices, \([T_{adj}^a, T_{adj}^b] = i \epsilon ^{abc} T_{adj}^c\).

To understand what these matrices do physically: in the fundamental representation, SU(2) matrices rotate fermion doublets (like quarks or leptons). In the adjoint representation, these \(3 \times 3\) matrices rotate the gauge bosons \(W_\mu ^1, W_\mu ^2, W_\mu ^3\) into one another. This is why, in the non-Abelian case, unlike QED, we have vertices involving three or four gauge bosons.

\(\nextSection \)

G Lagrangian Formalism

G.1 Lagrangian formulation of Classical Mechanics

This is a very short section with some minimal introduction to the formalism.

According to Newton’s second law, for a particle of mass \(m\) under a force \(\mathbf {F}\):

\begin{equation} \mathbf {F} = m\mathbf {a} \end{equation}
If the force is conservative, it can be expressed as the gradient of a scalar potential:
\begin{equation} \mathbf {F} = -\nabla U \end{equation}
in which case Newton’s second law reads
\begin{equation} m \frac {d\mathbf {v}}{dt} = -\nabla U \end{equation}

Classical mechanics can be formulated by starting with the Lagrangian

\begin{equation} L = T - U, \qquad T = \frac {1}{2} m \dot {\mathbf {q}}^2 \end{equation}
The Lagrangian is a function of the coordinates \(q_i\) (say, \(q_1 = x, q_2 = y, q_3 = z\)) and their time derivatives \(\dot {q}_i\) (\(\dot {q}_1 = v_x, \dot {q}_2 = v_y, \dot {q}_3 = v_z\)). In the Lagrangian formulation the fundamental law of motion is the Euler–Lagrange equation:
\begin{equation} \frac {d}{dt} \left ( \frac {\partial L}{\partial \dot {q}_i} \right ) = \frac {\partial L}{\partial q_i} \end{equation}
In Cartesian coordinates:
\begin{equation} \frac {\partial L}{\partial \dot {x}} = m\dot {x}, \qquad \frac {\partial L}{\partial x} = -\frac {\partial U}{\partial x} \end{equation}
which gives:
\begin{equation} m \ddot {x} = -\frac {\partial U}{\partial x} \end{equation}

There is another formulation that starts from the Hamiltonian

\begin{equation} H = \sum _i p_i \dot {q}_i - L \end{equation}
where
\begin{equation} p_i = \frac {\partial L}{\partial \dot {q}_i} \end{equation}
The dynamics in the Hamiltonian formulation are given by Hamilton’s equations of motion, which are a set of first-order differential equations:
\begin{equation} \dot {q}_i = \frac {\partial H}{\partial p_i}, \qquad \dot {p}_i = -\frac {\partial H}{\partial q_i} \end{equation}
For a particle in a potential:
\begin{equation} p = m\dot {x}, \qquad H = \frac {1}{2} m \dot {x}^2 + U(x) = T + U \end{equation}

Both the Lagrangian and Hamiltonian formulations generalise to continuous systems (fields), where \(q_i(t)\) becomes a field \(\phi (x,t)\). In relativistic theories, the Lagrangian formulation is typically preferred as a starting point because it can be written in a manifestly Lorentz-invariant form, treating space and time on equal footing. The Hamiltonian formalism instead singles out time, making covariance less explicit.

G.2 Euler-Lagrange Equations

The Lagrangian density \(\mathcal {L}(\phi _j,\partial _\mu \phi _j)\) determines the classical equations of motion through the principle of stationary action. The action is defined as

\begin{equation} S = \int d^4x \, \mathcal {L}, \end{equation}
and the physical trajectory is obtained by requiring that \(S\) be stationary under infinitesimal variations of the fields,
\begin{equation} \phi _j(x) \rightarrow \phi _j(x) + \delta \phi _j(x), \end{equation}
with the boundary condition that \(\delta \phi _j(x)\) vanishes on the boundary of the spacetime region on which \(S\) is evaluated. Typically, one evaluates \(S\) between two times \(t_i\) and \(t_f\), and over all of space, so this means that \(\delta \phi _j(x)\) vanishes sufficiently far away from the region of interest.

Under a variation of the fields, the variation of the action is

\begin{equation} \delta S = \int d^4x \sum _j \left [ \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _j}\,\delta \phi _j + \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \,\delta (\partial _\mu \phi _j) \right ]. \end{equation}
Using
\begin{equation} \delta (\partial _\mu \phi _j) = \partial _\mu (\delta \phi _j), \end{equation}
we obtain
\begin{equation} \delta S = \int d^4x \sum _j \left [ \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _j}\,\delta \phi _j + \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \,\partial _\mu (\delta \phi _j) \right ]. \end{equation}
Integrating the second term by parts yields
\begin{equation} \int d^4x \, \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \,\partial _\mu (\delta \phi _j) = \int d^4x \, \partial _\mu \left ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \,\delta \phi _j \right ) - \int d^4x \, \partial _\mu \left ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \right ) \delta \phi _j. \end{equation}
The first term is a boundary term, which vanishes since \(\delta \phi _j = 0\) on the boundary. Therefore,
\begin{equation} \delta S = \int d^4x \sum _j \left [ \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _j} - \partial _\mu \left ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \right ) \right ] \delta \phi _j. \end{equation}

If we require this to vanish for each and every arbitrary variation \(\delta \phi _j\), we obtain the Euler-Lagrange equations of motion:

\begin{equation} \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _j} - \partial _\mu \left ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _j)} \right )=0, \end{equation}
for each \(j\).

G.3 Noether’s Theorem

Consider a set of fields \(\phi _n(x)\) with a Lagrangian density \(\mathcal {L} (\phi _n, \partial _\mu \phi _n)\). Assume that under an infinitesimal transformation of the fields

\begin{equation} \phi _n(x) \to \phi _n'(x) = \phi _n(x) + \alpha \Delta \phi _n(x), \end{equation}
where \(\alpha \) is a continuous infinitesimal parameter and \(\Delta \phi _n(x)\) is the field deformation, the Lagrangian transforms as
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} \to \mathcal {L}' = \mathcal {L} + \alpha \, \partial _\mu \mathcal {K}^\mu , \end{equation}
for some function \(\mathcal {K}^\mu \) that leaves the action invariant.

Treating \(\mathcal {L}\) as a function of the fields \(\phi _n\) and their derivatives \(\partial _\mu \phi _n\) as independent variables, the variation \(\delta \mathcal {L}\) is given by the total differential:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \delta \mathcal {L} &= \mathcal {L}(\phi _n + \alpha \Delta \phi _n, \partial _\mu \phi _n + \alpha \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n)) - \mathcal {L}(\phi _n, \partial _\mu \phi _n) \\ &= \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _n} (\alpha \Delta \phi _n) + \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} (\alpha \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n)) \\ &= \alpha \sum _n \Big [ \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _n} \Delta \phi _n + \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n) \Big ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the Euler-Lagrange equations we obtain.
\begin{equation} \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _n} - \partial _\mu \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} = 0 \quad \implies \quad \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial \phi _n} = \partial _\mu \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)}, \end{equation}
Using
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \Bigg ( \frac {\partial L}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \, \Delta \phi _n \Bigg ) = \partial _\mu \Bigg ( \frac {\partial L}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Bigg ) \Delta \phi _n + \frac {\partial L}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \, \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n), \end{equation}
we substitute into \(\delta \mathcal {L}\):
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \delta \mathcal {L} &= \alpha \sum _n \Big [ \partial _\mu \Big ( \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n \Big ) - \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n) + \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \partial _\mu (\Delta \phi _n) \Big ] \\ &= \alpha \, \partial _\mu \Big ( \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n \Big ). \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the assumption \(\delta \mathcal {L} = \alpha \, \partial _\mu \mathcal {K}^\mu \), we obtain
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \Big ( \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n \Big ) = \partial _\mu \mathcal {K}^\mu , \end{equation}
or equivalently,
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \Big ( \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n - \mathcal {K}^\mu \Big ) = 0. \end{equation}
This leads to the conserved Noether current
\begin{equation} J^\mu = -\sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n + \mathcal {K}^\mu , \end{equation}
which satisfies
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu J^\mu = 0. \end{equation}
In the case of an internal global symmetry, where the Lagrangian density is invariant under the transformation, we have
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} \to \mathcal {L}' = \mathcal {L} \end{equation}
meaning
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \mathcal {K}^\mu = 0. \end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu \Big ( \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n \Big ) = 0. \end{equation}
and the conserved current reduces to
\begin{equation} J^\mu =- \sum _n \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi _n)} \Delta \phi _n. \end{equation}
G.3.1 Noether Current for Complex Klein-Gordon Field

Notice that we can write the complex Klein-Gordon Lagrangian

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = \partial _\mu \phi ^* \partial ^\mu \phi - m^2 \phi ^* \phi . \end{equation}
You can check that this also leads to the Klein-Gordon equation. This Lagrangian is invariant under the global phase transformation:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} \phi \rightarrow \phi ^\prime &= e^{i a Q}\phi , \\ \phi ^* \rightarrow \phi ^{\prime *} &= e^{-i a Q}\phi ^*. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Under an infinitesimal transformation, we have:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & \Delta \phi = i Q \phi , \\ & \Delta \phi ^* = -i Q \phi ^*. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using Noether’s procedure:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} & \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi )} = \partial ^\mu \phi ^*, \\ & \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi ^*)} = \partial ^\mu \phi . \end {aligned} \end{equation}
The resulting conserved Noether current is
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} J^\mu &= - \left [ \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi )} \Delta \phi + \frac {\partial \mathcal {L}}{\partial (\partial _\mu \phi ^*)} \Delta \phi ^* \right ] \\ &= - \left [ (\partial ^\mu \phi ^*)(i Q \phi ) + (\partial ^\mu \phi )(-i Q \phi ^*) \right ] \\ &= - i Q \left ( \phi \partial ^\mu \phi ^* - \phi ^* \partial ^\mu \phi \right ) \\ &= i Q \left ( \phi ^* \partial ^\mu \phi - \phi \partial ^\mu \phi ^* \right ) \end {aligned} \end{equation}
This current has a form identical to the one derived from the Klein-Gordon equation, but it has a different interpretation. We no longer treat \(\phi \) as a single-particle probability amplitude, which would lead to the problem of negative probability densities. Instead this Noether current is associated with the conservation of particle number (particles minus antiparticles) and electric charge.

\(\nextSection \)

H Feynman Rules

Here, as in the main body of the notes, I omit the formal QFT steps and take as starting point the following statement [18, 19]

This means that the propagators are always the inverse of the matrices in the bilinear terms of the Lagrangian, and the vertices are the coefficients in front of the remaining terms in the Lagrangian, regardless whether time derivatives occur or not.

H.1 Gauge Boson Propagators

The first step is to use a bit of calculus to bring the Lagrangian into the form \(\mathcal {L}= \frac {1}{2} \phi P \phi \). The trick is to use integration by parts for the kinetic term. Then we obtain a surface or boundary term. A surface term is a term involving a total divergence \(\partial _\mu \Lambda ^\mu \). According to the four-dimensional version of the Divergence Theorem (Gauss’s Theorem), the integral of a divergence over a volume \(V\) is equal to the flux through its boundary \(\partial V\):

\begin{equation} \int _{V} \partial _\mu \Lambda ^\mu \, d^4x = \oint _{\partial V} \Lambda ^\mu \, d\Sigma _\mu . \end{equation}

In perturbative quantum field theory, where one considers small fluctuations around a stable vacuum, the fields are assumed to be sufficiently well behaved at spacetime infinity, so that they, together with the derivatives appearing in \(\Lambda ^\mu \), fall off rapidly enough as \(|x|\to \infty \). Under this assumption, the flux through the boundary at infinity vanishes,

\begin{equation} \oint _{\partial V}\Lambda ^\mu \, d\Sigma _\mu = 0, \end{equation}
and therefore the surface term does not contribute to the action. .

Scalar Bosons For the Klein-Gordon field we have

\begin{equation} \partial _\mu (\phi \partial ^\mu \phi ) = (\partial _\mu \phi )(\partial ^\mu \phi ) + \phi (\partial _\mu \partial ^\mu \phi ). \end{equation}
Rearranging this we can obtain the kinetic term:
\begin{equation} (\partial _\mu \phi )(\partial ^\mu \phi ) = \partial _\mu (\phi \partial ^\mu \phi ) - \phi (\partial ^2 \phi ), \end{equation}
where \(\partial ^2 \equiv \partial _\mu \partial ^\mu \).

The integral of the kinetic term is

\begin{equation} \int d^4x \frac {1}{2} (\partial _\mu \phi ) (\partial ^\mu \phi ) = \int d^4x \frac {1}{2} \partial _\mu (\phi \partial ^\mu \phi ) - \int d^4x \frac {1}{2} \phi (\partial _\mu \partial ^\mu \phi ). \end{equation}
The first term on the right-hand side is a surface term.

Therefore, we have

\begin{equation} (\partial _\mu \phi )(\partial ^\mu \phi ) \to - \phi (\partial ^2 \phi ). \end{equation}
Including the mass term, we have the operator \(P = -(\partial ^2 + m^2)\). Applying the replacement \(\partial _\mu \to -i p_\mu \) (so \(\partial ^2 \to -p^2\)):
\begin{equation} P = -(-p^2 + m^2) = p^2 - m^2. \end{equation}
The propagator is \(i\) times the inverse of \(P\)
\begin{equation} \frac {i}{p^2 - m^2 + i\epsilon }, \end{equation}
where we also added the \(i\epsilon \) prescription.

Massive Vector Bosons For a massive vector boson \(A_\mu \) ( such as the \(W\) or \(Z\) bosons) the Lagrangian is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = -\frac {1}{4} F_{\mu \nu } F^{\mu \nu } + \frac {1}{2} M^2 A_\mu A^\mu , \end{equation}
where \(F_{\mu \nu } = \partial _\mu A_\nu - \partial _\nu A_\mu \). Expanding the kinetic term yields:
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{\text {kin}} = -\frac {1}{2} \left ( \partial _\mu A_\nu \partial ^\mu A^\nu - \partial _\mu A_\nu \partial ^\nu A^\mu \right ). \end{equation}

To bring this into the quadratic form \(\frac {1}{2} A_\nu P^{\nu \mu } A_\mu \), we perform integration by parts. Let’s look at the first term:

\begin{equation} -\frac {1}{2} \int d^4x (\partial _\mu A_\nu ) (\partial ^\mu A^\nu ). \end{equation}
Using the product rule
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu (A_\nu \partial ^\mu A^\nu ) = (\partial _\mu A_\nu )(\partial ^\mu A^\nu ) + A_\nu (\partial ^2 A^\nu ), \end{equation}
we can rewrite the integral as:
\begin{equation} -\frac {1}{2} \int d^4x \left [ \partial _\mu (A_\nu \partial ^\mu A^\nu ) - A_\nu (\partial ^2 A^\nu ) \right ]. \end{equation}
We identify the surface term:
\begin{equation} \underbrace {-\frac {1}{2} \int d^4x \partial _\mu (A_\nu \partial ^\mu A^\nu )} _{\text {Surface Term}} + \frac {1}{2} \int d^4x A_\nu (\partial ^2 A^\nu ). \end{equation}
Dropping the surface term we are left with:
\begin{equation} -\frac {1}{2} \int d^4x (\partial _\mu A_\nu ) (\partial ^\mu A^\nu ) = \frac {1}{2} \int d^4x A_\nu (\partial ^2 A^\nu ). \end{equation}

Similarly, for the second term:

\begin{equation} \frac {1}{2} \int d^4x (\partial _\mu A_\nu ) (\partial ^\nu A^\mu ) = -\frac {1}{2} \int d^4x A_\nu (\partial ^\nu \partial _\mu A^\mu ). \end{equation}
Combining these with the mass term, we have:
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L} = \frac {1}{2} A_\nu (\partial ^2 A^\nu ) - \frac {1}{2} A_\nu (\partial ^\nu \partial _\mu A^\mu ) + \frac {1}{2} M^2 A_\nu A^\nu . \end{equation}

To write this in the form \(\frac {1}{2} A_\mu P^{\mu \nu } A_\nu \), we perform the following index manipulations:

  • \(A_\nu \partial ^2 A^\nu \to A_\mu (g^{\mu \nu } \partial ^2) A_\nu \).

  • \(-A_\nu \partial ^\nu \partial _\mu A^\mu \to -A_\mu (\partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu ) A_\nu \).

  • \(M^2 A_\nu A^\nu \to A_\mu (g^{\mu \nu } M^2) A_\nu \).

Therefore, the operator is

\begin{equation} P^{\mu \nu } = g^{\mu \nu } (\partial ^2 + M^2) - \partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu . \end{equation}
In momentum space:
\begin{equation} P^{\mu \nu }(p) = g^{\mu \nu }(-p^2 + M^2) + p^\mu p^\nu . \end{equation}
Inverting this matrix and multiplying by \(i\) gives the propagator:
\begin{equation} \frac {-i \left (g_{\mu \nu } - \frac {p_\mu p_\nu }{M^2}\right )} {p^2 - M^2 + i\epsilon }, \end{equation}
where we also added the \(i\epsilon \) prescription.

Massless Vector Bosons For the photon (\(M=0\)), the operator is

\begin{equation} P_{\mu \nu }(q) = -q^2 g_{\mu \nu } + q_\mu q_\nu \end{equation}
The most general form for an inverse \(D^{\nu \lambda }\) that satisfies Lorentz covariance is:
\begin{equation} D^{\nu \lambda } = A(q^2) g^{\nu \lambda } + B(q^2) q^\nu q^\lambda . \end{equation}
If \(D\) is the inverse, then:
\begin{equation} P_{\mu \nu } D^{\nu \lambda } = \delta ^\lambda _\mu \end{equation}
Performing the multiplication:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} (A g_{\nu \lambda } + B q_\nu q_\lambda ) (-q^2 g_{\mu \nu } + q_\mu q_\nu ) = -A q^2 \delta ^\lambda _\mu + (A - B q^2 + B q^2) q_\mu q_\lambda \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Simplifying the terms, we arrive at:
\begin{equation} - A q^2 \delta ^\lambda _\mu + A q_\mu q_\lambda = \delta ^\lambda _\mu \end{equation}
The conditions for this to hold are: From the \(\delta ^\lambda _\mu \) term:
\begin{equation} A = -\frac {1}{q^2} \end{equation}
and from the \(q_\mu q_\lambda \) term:
\begin{equation} A = 0 \end{equation}
Because \(A\) cannot be both \(-1/q^2\) and \(0\), this inverse does not exist. This is consequence of gauge invariance.

We therefore add a gauge fixing term:

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{\text {gf}} = -\frac {1}{2\xi } (\partial _\mu A^\mu )^2. \end{equation}
We have
\begin{equation} I_{\text {gf}} = -\frac {1}{2\xi } \int d^4x (\partial _\mu A^\mu )(\partial _\nu A^\nu ). \end{equation}
Using the product rule:
\begin{equation} \partial _\mu (A^\mu \partial _\nu A^\nu ) = (\partial _\mu A^\mu )(\partial _\nu A^\nu ) + A^\mu \partial _\mu (\partial _\nu A^\nu ), \end{equation}
and rearranging we obtain
\begin{equation} I_{\text {gf}} = -\frac {1}{2\xi } \int d^4x \left [ \partial _\mu (A^\mu \partial _\nu A^\nu ) - A_\mu (\partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu A_\nu ) \right ]. \end{equation}
The first term is a surface term. Dropping it we are left with:
\begin{equation} I_{\text {gf}} = \frac {1}{2\xi } \int d^4x A_\mu (\partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu ) A_\nu . \end{equation}

Using the results from the massive case the total operator becomes:

\begin{equation} P^{\mu \nu } = \partial ^2 g^{\mu \nu } - \partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu + \frac {1}{\xi } \partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu = \partial ^2 g^{\mu \nu } - \left (1 - \frac {1}{\xi }\right ) \partial ^\mu \partial ^\nu . \end{equation}
In momentum space:
\begin{equation} P^{\mu \nu } = - p^2 g^{\mu \nu } + \left (1 - \frac {1}{\xi }\right ) p^\mu p^\nu . \end{equation}
The propagator is \(i\) times the inverse of this operator:
\begin{equation} \frac {-i}{p^2 + i\epsilon } \left [ g_{\mu \nu } - (1 - \xi ) \frac {p_\mu p_\nu }{p^2} \right ], \end{equation}
where we also added the \(i\epsilon \) prescription.

In the Feynman gauge (\(\xi =1\)), this reduces to:

\begin{equation} \frac {-i g_{\mu \nu }}{p^2 + i\epsilon }, \end{equation}

H.2 QCD Interaction Feynman Rules

Quark-Gluon Vertex The interaction term in the QCD Lagrangian is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{q\bar {q}g} = g_s \, \bar {\psi }_i \, \gamma ^\mu (t^a)_{ij} \, \psi _j \, G_\mu ^a. \end{equation}
Including the factor \(i\) for the Feynman rule gives
\begin{equation} i \mathcal {L}_{\text {int}} = i g_s \, \bar {\psi } \gamma ^\mu t^a \psi \, G_\mu ^a. \end{equation}
Stripping off the fields leaves the vertex:
\begin{equation} i g_s \gamma ^\mu t^a = i g_s \gamma ^\mu \frac {\lambda ^a}{2}. \end{equation}

Three-Gluon Vertex The kinetic term in the QCD Lagrangian is

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{\text {kin}} = -\frac {1}{4} G_{\mu \nu }^a G^{a\,\mu \nu } \end{equation}
with
\begin{equation} G_{\mu \nu }^a = \underbrace {\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a - \partial _\nu G_\mu ^a} _{\text {Abelian}} - \underbrace {g_s f^{abc} G_\mu ^b G_\nu ^c} _{\text {non-Abelian}}. \end{equation}
Expanding the square gives the cross-term for three-gluon interaction:
\begin{equation} -\frac {2}{4} g_s f^{abc} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a - \partial _\nu G_\mu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}. \end{equation}
\begin{equation} -\frac {2}{4} \big [ g_s f^{abc} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c} -g_s f^{abc} (\partial _\nu G_\mu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}]. \end{equation}
For the second term we rename the colours being summed over:
\begin{equation} T_2 = f^{acb} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu c} G^{\nu b}. \end{equation}
Accounting for the antisymmetry \(f^{acb} = - f^{abc}\) and the symmetry of the fields \(G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}\) under \(b \leftrightarrow c\):
\begin{equation} f^{acb} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu c} G^{\nu b} = - f^{abc} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}. \end{equation}
Therefore,
\begin{equation} T_2 = - f^{abc} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}. \end{equation}
Leading to:
\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{3g} = - g_s f^{abc} (\partial _\mu G_\nu ^a) G^{\mu b} G^{\nu c}. \end{equation}

We do the standard replacement \(\partial _\mu \to -i p_\mu \), and we label the gluons as \((a,\mu ,p_1), (b,\nu ,p_2), (c, \sigma ,p_3)\) to obtain the following permutations:

\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} S^{abc}_{\mu \nu \sigma }(p_1,p_2,p_3) &= - g_s f^{abc} \Big [ g^{\mu \nu } (p_1 - p_2)^\sigma \\ &\quad + g^{\nu \sigma } (p_2 - p_3)^\mu \\ &\quad + g^{\sigma \mu } (p_3 - p_1)^\nu \Big ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
Using the antisymmetry of \(f^{abc}\) (\(f^{bca} = f^{abc}\), \(f^{cab} = f^{abc}\)), this simplifies to:
\begin{equation} - g_s f^{abc} \Big [ g^{\mu \nu } (p_1 - p_2)^\sigma + g^{\nu \sigma } (p_2 - p_3)^\mu + g^{\sigma \mu } (p_3 - p_1)^\nu \Big ]. \end{equation}

Four-Gluon Vertex From the Lagrangian

\begin{equation} \mathcal {L}_{4g} = -\frac {1}{4} g_s^2 f^{abc} f^{ade} G_\mu ^b G_\nu ^c G^{\mu d} G^{\nu e}. \end{equation}
Using the symmetry of \(f^{abc}\), the \(4! = 24\) permutations reduce to three distinct colour pairings. Including \(i\), the four-gluon vertex is:
\begin{equation} \begin {aligned} -i g_s^2 \Big [ & f^{abe} f^{cde} (g^{\mu \rho } g^{\nu \sigma } - g^{\mu \sigma } g^{\nu \rho }) \\ +& f^{ace} f^{bde} (g^{\mu \nu } g^{\rho \sigma } - g^{\mu \sigma } g^{\nu \rho }) \\ +& f^{ade} f^{bce} (g^{\mu \nu } g^{\rho \sigma } - g^{\mu \rho } g^{\nu \sigma }) \Big ]. \end {aligned} \end{equation}
